This is probably the most honest AI architecture breakdown on the internet right now.

9-layer AI production architecture :

→ services/ - RAG pipeline, semantic cache, memory, query rewriter, router. Not one file. Five.

→ agents/ - document grader, decomposer, adaptive router. Self-correcting by design.

→ prompts/ - versioned, typed, registered. Never hardcoded.

→ security/ - input, content, output. Three guards, not one.

→ evaluation/ - golden dataset, offline eval, online monitor. Most people skip this entire layer and ship blind.

→ observability/ - per-stage tracing, feedback linked to traces, cost per query.

→ .claude/ - agent context so your AI coding assistant knows the codebase before it touches a file



Two years ago, backend engineering looked very different.

Most of the work was around:
APIs
Databases
Queues
Caching
Auth
Cloud
Security
Observability
That foundation is still extremely important.
But now, a new layer is being added on top of it.

LLM APIs.
Embeddings.
Vector databases.
RAG.
Context engineering.
Tool calling.
Model routing.
Agents.
Memory.
Evals.
Guardrails.
Human review.
Agent observability.

This is why many engineers feel like the role is changing so fast.
It is not because backend engineering is becoming less relevant.
It is because backend engineering is becoming the foundation of production AI.

Calling an LLM API is easy.
Building a reliable AI system around that model is the hard part.

You need to think about:
How context enters the system
How tools are selected
How memory is managed
How permissions are enforced
How outputs are evaluated
How failures are traced
How humans stay in the loop
How cost and latency are controlled
The model is only one part.
The real engineering is the harness around it.

My simple view:
Phase 1 was prompt engineering.
Phase 2 was context engineering.
Phase 3 is AI systems engineering.

The next generation of high-value engineers will not just build applications.
They will build AI systems that can retrieve, reason, act, recover, and be trusted in production.

Backend engineering is not dead.
It is evolving into AI systems engineering.


Generating apps with AI is no longer the hard part.
You can open Claude Code, Cursor, Lovable, or any modern AI builder and generate an internal app from a prompt, screenshot, CSV, or rough idea in minutes.

That is incredible.
But a prototype is not production.

Once that AI-generated app touches real business data, the questions change:
Does it have RBAC?
Does it support SSO?
Who can access the data?
Where are the audit logs?
Can IT see what is running?
Can security trace what queries were executed?
Can the app safely connect to Postgres, Salesforce, Snowflake, or internal APIs?

This is where the “vibe-coded” app movement hits the enterprise wall.

According to Retool’s 2026 AI Governance Report:

→ 93% of tech leaders are worried about vibe-coded apps in production
→ Around 95% of organizations have production visibility gaps
→ 59% can’t confirm whether an AI-generated tool has caused a live production incident
→ Only 8% describe their AI governance as strong

That tells us something important:

AI has made app creation cheap and fast.
But production still requires governance.
This is what I found interesting about @retool’s new launch: a rebuilt AI-native app builder that lets teams build anywhere — Claude Code, Cursor, a screenshot, an existing React app — and run through Retool's governed platform when it's time to ship.


Builders can continue using the AI tools they already love for fast prototyping and iteration.
But when it is time to move from prototype to production, Retool becomes the governed landing zone.

That means every AI-generated app inherits:
→ Real data connections
→ SSO
→ RBAC
→ Audit logs
→ Permission layers
→ Query transparency
→ Execution history
→ Inspectable React-based apps
This is the part enterprises care about.

Not just:
“Can AI build the app?”
But:
Can the app be governed?
Can it be audited?
Can it be secured?
Can it be trusted in production?

The next wave of AI app development will not be only about faster generation.
It will be about controlled deployment.

Because in the enterprise, speed without visibility becomes risk.

And governance without developer speed becomes friction.

The real win is bringing both together.

I partnered with @retool on this post because this is exactly the production gap many AI teams are running into right now.

Here’s how AI changes the game for introverts:

A lot of strong thinkers are not short on ideas.

They are short on speed, structure, and visibility.

They know what they think.

But the workplace rewards people who can turn thinking into emails, updates, proposals, meetings, decisions, and content fast.

AI helps close that gap.

Here’s the breakdown:

• Before meetings

Most introverts think better with time to process.

Use AI to:
→ turn rough thoughts into talking points
→ prepare answers to likely questions
→ rehearse difficult conversations
→ reduce the blank-mind effect in live rooms

Why it matters:
↳ you show up clearer before the pressure starts

• Writing and communication

Good judgment often gets hidden inside messy notes.

Use AI to:
→ clean up emails
→ sharpen proposals
→ summarise long threads
→ turn scattered ideas into structured updates

Why it matters:
↳ people understand your thinking faster

• Business storytelling

Technical people often know the value but struggle to explain it simply.

Use AI to:
→ translate technical work into business impact
→ simplify complex ideas
→ create cleaner slides
→ connect the work to cost, speed, risk, or revenue

Why it matters:
↳ your work becomes easier for leaders to act on

• Decision-making

Quiet people often see problems early but wait too long to say them.

Use AI to:
→ frame tradeoffs
→ compare options
→ write decision memos
→ explain the risk in plain language

Why it matters:
↳ your judgment becomes visible before the mistake happens

• Output speed

Context switching kills deep thinkers.

Use AI to:
→ draft first versions
→ organise messy research
→ repurpose one idea into many formats
→ remove the empty-page problem

Why it matters:
↳ one hour can produce what used to take half a day

The real advantage is simple:

→ clearer thinking
→ faster output
→ better preparation
→ less friction
→ more visible judgment

AI will not make introverts louder.

It makes their thinking easier to see.

And in a workplace built around speed, that matters.

• Mobile Apps: Android, iOS, Windows
• Payment Front: Bill payments, transfers
• Cashier Terminals: Branch operations
• ATM Software: Cash dispensing
• Chat Bots: 24/7 customer support

𝟐. 𝐌𝐈𝐃𝐃𝐋𝐄𝐖𝐀𝐑𝐄 (𝐈𝐝𝐞𝐧𝐭𝐢𝐭𝐲 𝐆𝐚𝐭𝐞𝐰𝐚𝐲):
• Authentication & Authorization APIs
• Banking API for transactions
• Access Manager: OAuth2, MFA, SSO
• Server DBP: Core banking services
• Recurring Payments Service
• Anti-Fraud: Risk prevention
• Operation Server: Account management
• Monitoring Server: Health checks

𝟑. 𝐁𝐀𝐂𝐊𝐄𝐍𝐃 (𝐄𝐒𝐁 𝐈𝐧𝐭𝐞𝐠𝐫𝐚𝐭𝐢𝐨𝐧):
• Card switch, CRM, Antifraud systems
• Core banking, payment aggregators
• 3rd party gateways, service providers
• ISO 8583 compliance

𝟒. 𝐂𝐫𝐢𝐭𝐢𝐜𝐚𝐥 𝐏𝐫𝐢𝐧𝐜𝐢𝐩𝐥𝐞𝐬:
- Security: Multi-layer auth, encryption, PCI-DSS
- Scalability: Microservices, horizontal scaling
- Reliability: Redundancy, disaster recovery
- Compliance: Audit trails, KYC/AML
- Integration: Open APIs, ESB, adapters

Common Mistakes:
• Monolithic architecture
• Weak authentication
• No fraud detection
• Tight coupling
• Missing audit trails

Neither your strategy nor the AI technology is the constraint.
Your leadership operating system is.

🚀 Behind the Scenes of UPI: How It Handles ₹18.41 Trillion Transactions!

India's UPI has revolutionised digital payments - an interoperable, public infrastructure enabling private innovation. It's simple, fast, and widely adopted.

🔹 How UPI Works
 UPI links virtual payment addresses (VPAs) like rahul@hdfc directly to bank accounts, eliminating the need for IFSC codes. But what happens behind the scenes when you send money?

Let’s break it down:
1️⃣ Rahul (Sender) uses PhonePe to send ₹100 to Amit (Receiver).
 2️⃣ He enters Amit’s UPI ID.
 3️⃣ PhonePe encrypts the request and forwards it to its acquiring bank (SBI).
 4️⃣ SBI sends it to NPCI, which validates Rahul’s account and fetches Amit’s bank details.
 5️⃣ NPCI routes the request to Amit’s bank (ICICI) for validation.
 6️⃣ ICICI confirms the details, and NPCI authorizes Rahul’s bank (HDFC) to debit ₹100.
 7️⃣ HDFC checks balances, debits the amount, and confirms back to NPCI.
 8️⃣ NPCI instructs ICICI to credit ₹100 to Amit’s account.
 9️⃣ ICICI completes the credit and notifies NPCI.
 🔟 NPCI ensures settlements between banks via RBI, completing the transaction.
All of this happens in seconds!

💡 Why UPI Works So Well
 ✅ Banks: Hold funds & process transactions
 ✅ Payment Apps: Link bank accounts & facilitate transactions
 ✅ NPCI: The trusted switch ensuring seamless routing & security
This blend of innovation and security has made UPI the backbone of India’s digital economy. 🌍

Agentic AI Is the New Operating System for Enterprises

After spending weeks researching enterprise AI systems, autonomous workflows, and production-grade agents, one thing became very clear:

We are moving beyond "AI tools"
…into the era of Agentic AI systems.

This shift is massive.

Most people still think AI is:
➡️ prompts
➡️ chatbots
➡️ content generation

But the next generation of AI is about systems that can:

• perceive
• reason
• plan
• act
• use tools
• collaborate
• learn from feedback
• continuously improve

That's what Agentic AI really means.

The framework in this visual perfectly captures how modern AI architecture is evolving into layered intelligence systems.

At the center of everything are:
✅ Foundation Models / LLMs
✅ Memory Systems
✅ Tool Use & APIs
✅ Orchestration Layers
✅ Autonomous Workflows

And this is important:

The real value of AI no longer comes from the model alone.

It comes from the system around the model.

That includes:

• Retrieval pipelines (RAG)
• Multi-agent coordination
• Runtime orchestration
• Long-term memory
• Observability
• Feedback loops
• Safety guardrails
• Policy enforcement

This is exactly why enterprises are now investing heavily in:
→ AI orchestration platforms
→ agent runtimes
→ vector databases
→ workflow engines
→ memory architectures
→ governance layers

Because production AI is not a single prompt.

It's an ecosystem.

One of the biggest misconceptions I see right now is companies trying to deploy "agents" without:
❌ memory
❌ evaluation
❌ orchestration
❌ monitoring
❌ rollback systems
❌ safety constraints

That's not Agentic AI.

That's just automation with risk.

The future belongs to organisations that can build:

• reliable agents
• scalable workflows
• adaptive systems
• human-AI collaboration layers

And perhaps the most exciting part?

We're still early.

What we're seeing today is probably the "basic internet phase" of Agentic AI.

Over the next few years, we'll likely see:

• self-improving enterprise agents
• collaborative multi-agent ecosystems
• autonomous research systems
• AI operating layers across businesses

The companies learning this architecture now will have a huge advantage later.

Because in the future, competitive advantage won't just come from data.

It will come from:
how intelligently your agents can reason, retrieve, coordinate, and execute.

Agentic AI is no longer experimental.
It's becoming enterprise infrastructure.

What layer of this framework do you think is the hardest to get right in production?


Prompt engineering is not the hard part.
Production is.

Building an AI demo is simple.
1 prompt
1 user
Looks impressive.

Production changes the game.

Now you have:
→ latency problems
→ hallucinations
→ security risks
→ retrieval failures
→ evaluation gaps
→ agent failure loops
→ cost explosions

This is where AI projects stop being “LLM apps.”
And start becoming engineering systems.

The shift many teams miss:
LLMs are only one component.
The real work is designing the system around them.

You need to think about:
LLMs → Prompting → Production → Security → Evaluation → RAG → Agents
Not as isolated topics.
As one connected stack.

Good AI systems are not built by stacking frameworks.

They are built by understanding tradeoffs.
When to retrieve.
When to cache.
When to constrain.
When NOT to use an agent.
When accuracy matters more than creativity.

3 Levels of Claude

𝗕𝗲𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗲 𝗯𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗮𝘁 𝗔𝗜 𝗶𝗻 𝗷𝘂𝘀𝘁 𝟭 𝗺𝗶𝗻𝘂𝘁𝗲 𝗮 𝗱𝗮𝘆.
𝗚𝗲𝘁 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗔𝗜 𝗻𝗲𝘄𝘀𝗹𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝘀𝗺𝗮𝗿𝘁 𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗱𝗲𝗿𝘀 𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗱.
𝗦𝗶𝗴𝗻 𝘂𝗽 𝗳𝗿𝗲𝗲 𝗻𝗼𝘄 → aiforleaders.com

__________

If you're only using Claude as a chatbot, you're using it wrong.

There are 3 levels to Claude:
→ Claude Chat
→ Claude Cowork
→ Claude Code

Each one has a different purpose and use case.

And each one gets more useful as you integrate it into your content workflow.

Here's a breakdown of each level and how you can use it:

Level 1: Claude Chat

My favourite part about Claude is Claude projects.

You can create multiple projects in it and feed them a significant amount of information about your brand and business.

The downside to Claude Chat is its processing speed.

It's slower than ChatGPT 5.5.

But for in-depth writing that needs a lot of context, it gets the job done.

Best for:
→ Detailed copywriting projects
→ Storing in-depth context
→ Connecting no-code tools

Level 2: Claude Cowork

This is Claude's agentic feature.

It can handle tasks inside your workflow without you doing everything manually.

But there are some downsides.

Cowork's credit usage is pretty high, and accepting all of its permission requests can be pretty tedious.

You can set Claude to act on its own.

But if it misunderstands your instructions, it can make mistakes.

Best for:
→ File and task management
→ Automating repetitive tasks
→ Desktop workflow automation

Level 3: Claude Code

This is where Claude becomes seriously useful for your content systems.

You can use Claude Code in your desktop app.

Or through its extension in Terminal or VS Code.

The main downside with Claude Code is that there's a learning curve.

If you're not familiar with vibe coding or how to integrate GitHub repos...

It takes time to figure it out.

But once you do, it can 10x your content creation efficiency.

Best for:
→ Integrating tools with no connectors
→ Large-scale coding projects
→ Building advanced content workflows

Don't stop learning Claude at level one.

Learn all 3 to effectively integrate each one into your content workflow.

Which level of Claude are you currently at?

Let me know in the comments.

This question around RAG vs Fine Tuning is very important for any AI interviews.

A company wants an AI support assistant.
→ 50,000 historical support tickets
→ New tickets added every month
→ AI budget: $10,000 / month

Fine-tune a model? Build RAG? Or both?

The wrong answer is jumping straight to "fine-tune it on the 50k tickets."

Here's why that's usually a trap — and how I'd actually reason through it. 🧵

🔴 Why fine-tuning alone fails here
- Knowledge changes monthly → you'd retrain constantly → cost + ops nightmare on a $10k budget.
- Fine-tuning teaches STYLE and FORMAT well, but it's a poor way to inject FACTS.
- No citations → in support, "where did this answer come from?" matters.

🟢 Why RAG is the strong default
- New tickets? Just embed and add them to the vector store. No retraining.
- Answers stay grounded in real tickets → fewer hallucinations + source citations.
- Fits the budget: embedding + retrieval is cheap vs. repeated fine-tuning runs.

🟡 When "both" wins
- RAG for the knowledge (the 50k + monthly tickets).
- Light fine-tuning (or few-shot) ONLY for tone, response format, and domain phrasing.
- This hybrid gives you fresh facts AND a consistent support voice.

The factors that actually drive the decision:
1. How often does the knowledge change? (Monthly → favors RAG)
2. Is it a knowledge problem or a behavior problem? (Facts → RAG, Style → fine-tune)
3. Budget & ops maturity ($10k/mo → avoid frequent retraining)
4. Need for citations / auditability (Support → yes → RAG)
5. Latency & data volume (50k is small → RAG indexes easily)

My pick: RAG-first, with optional light fine-tuning for tone — not fine-tuning the knowledge.

𝟮𝟵 𝗖𝗟𝗔𝗨𝗗𝗘 𝗦𝗛𝗢𝗥𝗧𝗖𝗨𝗧𝗦

 𝗦𝗔𝗩𝗘 𝗧𝗛𝗜𝗦 𝗕𝗘𝗙𝗢𝗥𝗘 𝗬𝗢𝗨 𝗙𝗢𝗥𝗚𝗘𝗧

If you're using AI and not using these shortcuts, you're missing out on better prompts and better results.

1. 𝗔𝗖𝗧 𝗔𝗦 — makes Claude speak in a specific role.

2. 𝗧𝗟𝗗𝗥 — summarizes a very long text in a few lines.

3. 𝗖𝗛𝗔𝗜𝗡 𝗢𝗙 𝗧𝗛𝗢𝗨𝗚𝗛𝗧 — shows intermediate reasoning.

4. 𝗘𝗟𝗜𝟱 — is used to explain as if to a 5-year-old.

5. 𝗙𝗢𝗥𝗠𝗔𝗧 𝗔𝗦 — enforces a specific format (table, XML, JSON, etc.).

6. 𝗕𝗥𝗜𝗘𝗙𝗟𝗬 — forces a very short answer.

7. 𝗦𝗧𝗘𝗣-𝗕𝗬-𝗦𝗧𝗘𝗣 — lays out reasoning step by step.

8. 𝗔𝗨𝗗𝗜𝗘𝗡𝗖𝗘 — adapts the response to a chosen audience.

9. 𝗖𝗛𝗘𝗖𝗞𝗟𝗜𝗦𝗧 — turns a response into a checklist.

10. 𝗧𝗢𝗡𝗘 — changes the tone (formal, witty, empathetic, etc.).

11. 𝗙𝗜𝗥𝗦𝗧 𝗣𝗥𝗜𝗡𝗖𝗜𝗣𝗟𝗘𝗦 — rebuilds from fundamental basics.

12. 𝗦𝗪𝗢𝗧 — produces a strengths/weaknesses/opportunities/threats analysis.

13. 𝗖𝗢𝗠𝗣𝗔𝗥𝗘 — puts two or more things side by side.

14. 𝗘𝗫𝗘𝗖 𝗦𝗨𝗠𝗠𝗔𝗥𝗬 — gives a quick executive-style summary.

15. 𝗝𝗔𝗥𝗚𝗢𝗡 — asks to use technical vocabulary.

16. 𝗥𝗘𝗪𝗥𝗜𝗧𝗘 𝗔𝗦 — rephrases in a requested style.

17. 𝗗𝗘𝗩 𝗠𝗢𝗗𝗘 — simulates a raw, technical developer style.

18. 𝗠𝗨𝗟𝗧𝗜-𝗣𝗘𝗥𝗦𝗣𝗘𝗖𝗧𝗜𝗩𝗘 — shows several points of view.

19. 𝗡𝗢 𝗔𝗨𝗧𝗢𝗣𝗜𝗟𝗢𝗧 — forbids superficial, autopilot responses.

20. 𝗣𝗠 𝗠𝗢𝗗𝗘 — gives a project-management perspective.

21. 𝗦𝗖𝗛𝗘𝗠𝗔 — generates a structured outline or a data model.

22. 𝗖𝗢𝗡𝗧𝗘𝗫𝗧 𝗦𝗧𝗔𝗖𝗞 — keeps multiple layers of context in memory.

23. 𝗥𝗘𝗙𝗟𝗘𝗖𝗧𝗜𝗩𝗘 𝗠𝗢𝗗𝗘 — prompts Claude to reflect on its own answer.

24. 𝗕𝗘𝗚𝗜𝗡 𝗪𝗜𝗧𝗛 / 𝗘𝗡𝗗 𝗪𝗜𝗧𝗛 — forces starting or ending with something.

25. 𝗗𝗘𝗟𝗜𝗕𝗘𝗥𝗔𝗧𝗘 𝗧𝗛𝗜𝗡𝗞𝗜𝗡𝗚 — forces slower, more thoughtful reasoning.

26. 𝗘𝗩𝗔𝗟-𝗦𝗘𝗟𝗙 — asks for a critical self-evaluation of the response.

27. 𝗥𝗢𝗟𝗘: 𝗧𝗔𝗦𝗞: 𝗙𝗢𝗥𝗠𝗔𝗧: — explicitly defines the role, the task, and the expected format.

28. 𝗣𝗔𝗥𝗔𝗟𝗟𝗘𝗟 𝗟𝗘𝗡𝗦𝗘𝗦 — examines from several angles in parallel.

But only 5% are doing it in a way that will actually compound.

They experiment without using the right frameworks.

Most teams don't think about this at all.

Experimentation doesn't.

🧠 If Artificial Intelligence Had a Human Bod,  What Would Each Part Be?

We often talk about AI as if it's magic.
But behind every AI system is a surprisingly human-like architecture.
Think of it this way:
👀 Eyes → Computer Vision
AI sees the world through cameras, images, videos, and visual data.
🧠 Brain → Neural Networks + LLMs
The decision-making center that learns patterns, reasons, predicts, and generates content.
👂 Ears → Natural Language Processing & Audio AI
The ability to hear, understand, and respond to human speech.
👃 Nose → Sensors & Signal Detection
From electronic olfaction to environmental sensing, AI can detect patterns humans can't.
❤️ Heart → Cloud Infrastructure & Compute Power
The engine keeping everything alive, processing billions of calculations every second.
🤲 Hands → Robotics & Actuators
Where intelligence becomes action in the physical world.
🦴 Skeleton → Hardware Architecture
The structural foundation supporting every AI capability.
🧬 DNA → Training Data & Model Weights
The core blueprint that shapes how an AI system behaves and what it knows.
🩸 Blood → Data Pipelines & APIs
The continuous flow of information that keeps AI connected and functioning.
☁️ Subconscious → Latent Space

Perhaps the most fascinating part, the hidden representations where concepts, relationships, and patterns emerge.
The biggest misconception about AI is that it's a single technology.
In reality, AI is an ecosystem.
Models are only the brain.

Without data, infrastructure, sensors, software, and feedback loops, intelligence can't exist.
That's why the future won't be built by AI alone.

It will be built by the convergence of:
🔹 Data
🔹 Compute
🔹 Models
🔹 Infrastructure
🔹 Robotics
🔹 Human creativity

The more you understand the anatomy of AI, the better you'll understand where the next wave of innovation is coming from.

Which "body part" of AI do you think is advancing the fastest right now: Vision, LLMs, Robotics, or something else?

MCP vs A2A vs ANP vs ACP vs Agora

When it comes to agent communication & coordination, each protocol/framework has a unique role. Here’s a quick breakdown:

🔹 MCP (Model Context Protocol)
-Focus: Context management between AI models

-Best for: Chatbots integrating external tools

-Strength: Simple request-response, low latency

-Limitation: Small-scale, basic security

🔹 A2A (Agent-to-Agent)
-Focus: Direct communication between autonomous agents

-Best for: Multi-agent negotiation & task delegation

-Strength: TLS-secured async messaging

-Limitation: Moderate scalability & peer lookup delays

🔹 ANP (Agent Network Protocol)
-Focus: Large-scale decentralized agent networks

-Best for: IoT & distributed sensor systems

-Strength: High scalability & fault tolerance via decentralization

-Limitation: Variable latency due to multi-hop broadcasts

🔹 ACP (Agent Communication Protocol)
-Focus: Modular, broker-mediated messaging

-Best for: Enterprise workflows requiring compliance & extensibility

-Strength: Scales with broker load balancing, supports MQTT/AMQP

-Limitation: Broker dependency for reliability

🔹 Agora
-Focus: Real-time voice/video/data communication

-Best for: Video conferencing, live streaming, interactive apps

-Strength: Ultra-low latency, end-to-end encrypted media

-Limitation: Tailored for real-time media, not general agent workflows

🔷 Key Takeaway
-MCP → Small-scale AI integrations

-A2A → Peer-to-peer autonomous agents

-ANP → Decentralized IoT networks

-ACP → Enterprise modular workflows

-Agora → Real-time media at scale

Each serves a different layer of the agent ecosystem.
Choosing the right one depends on whether you need context, autonomy, scale, compliance, or real-time interaction.


Most people are using Claude like Google.

Here’s the 7-day system to make Claude useful for real work:

Most people stay stuck at basic prompts.

They ask one-off questions.
They start from scratch every time.
They never build a repeatable setup.

Claude gets much better when you stop treating it like a search box.

Here’s the breakdown:

• Day One to Two: Stop using Chat for everything

Chat is fine for quick questions.

But it is not where the real work happens.

Use Claude like this:
→ Chat for quick answers
→ Projects for ongoing work
→ Cowork for deeper execution

↳ Most people never leave Chat, so they miss the best parts of Claude.

• Day Two to Three: Build your Claude OS

Create a simple folder structure.

Use:
→ ABOUT ME for your identity, tone, and writing rules
→ PROJECTS for active work
→ TEMPLATES for repeatable formats
→ OUTPUTS for finished drafts and deliverables

↳ Random prompting becomes a working system.

• Day Three to Four: Replace prompts with files

Stop rewriting your instructions every time.

Start with two files:
→ about-me.md
→ anti-ai-style.md

↳ These give Claude memory, taste, and boundaries before the task starts.

• Day Four to Five: Let Claude think with you

Stop giving Claude tiny tasks only.

Ask it to:
→ generate options
→ rank ideas
→ compare approaches
→ plan execution

↳ You make better calls when Claude does the first round of thinking.

• Day Five to Six: Add tools

Connect Claude to the places where your work already lives.

Start with:
→ Google Docs
→ Slack
→ Notion

↳ Claude becomes more useful when it can work with your real context.

• Day Six to Seven: Automate the repeatable work

Start small.

Use Claude to:
→ schedule recurring tasks
→ run repeat workflows
→ prepare work before you start your day

↳ The goal is to wake up with work already moving.

Avoid these mistakes:

❌ Asking random one-off questions forever
❌ Treating every prompt like a fresh start
❌ Using Chat when the work belongs in Projects or Cowork

Build one layer at a time.

Claude only becomes powerful when your system around it gets better.

Start with Day One.

Which layer are you building first?

→ 𝐓𝐡𝐞 𝐀𝐈 𝐒𝐤𝐢𝐥𝐥𝐬 𝐓𝐡𝐚𝐭 𝐖𝐢𝐥𝐥 𝐒𝐞𝐩𝐚𝐫𝐚𝐭𝐞 𝐖𝐢𝐧𝐧𝐞𝐫𝐬 𝐢𝐧 2026

The future of AI isn’t about tools. It’s about skills that turn AI into impact. Here’s what will define leaders next year:

• Vibe Coding & AI Automation – Build faster with AI copilots. Replace manual workflows while keeping processes safe, compliant, and cost-controlled.

• AI Governance – Ensure enterprise AI runs responsibly. Manage risk, compliance, and performance in regulated industries.

• Prompt Architecture & RAG Systems – Create structured, reusable AI instructions. Connect AI to real, up-to-date data for consistent, scalable outputs across teams.

• Multimodal AI – Work seamlessly across text, image, audio, and video. Enable rich, interactive AI experiences that answer questions with private knowledge.

• AI Agents & Autonomous AI Workers – Execute multi-step tasks with minimal human intervention. Automate business and operational processes reliably.

• AI Observability & Production Scaling – Detect failures before users do. Monitor quality, latency, hallucinations, and drift while running AI at enterprise scale.

The common thread: speed, reliability, and value creation. Those who master these skills won’t just use AI-they’ll lead with it.

Fighting Financial Fraud with AI: Beyond Rules and Alerts
Financial fraud continues to evolve faster than traditional detection systems.
Fraudsters adapt.
Patterns change.
New attack methods emerge every day.
One of the most interesting projects I worked on involved developing an AI-driven Financial Fraud Detection framework designed to identify suspicious activities hidden within millions of transactions.
The challenge was straightforward:
How do you detect fraud when historical patterns are constantly changing?
Traditional rule-based systems are effective for known fraud scenarios, but they often struggle to identify emerging threats.
To address this challenge, we combined multiple analytical approaches:
✔ Machine Learning Models
✔ Network & Relationship Analytics
✔ Behavioral Pattern Analysis
✔ Anomaly Detection
✔ Risk Scoring Frameworks
✔ Real-Time Monitoring
The most fascinating insight was that fraud is rarely an isolated event.
It often exists within a network of relationships, transactions, devices, accounts, merchants, and behavioral patterns.
By moving beyond transaction-level analysis and examining interconnected patterns, we were able to uncover hidden risks that traditional approaches might miss.
Some key lessons from the project:
🔹 Data quality is often more important than algorithm complexity.
🔹 Explainability is critical for business adoption.
🔹 Fraud detection requires continuous learning and adaptation.
🔹 Combining domain expertise with AI delivers stronger results than technology alone.
As AI continues to mature, the future of fraud prevention will move from reactive detection toward proactive risk prediction and intelligent decision support.
The goal is no longer just to detect fraud.
The goal is to prevent it before it happens.
What do you think will have the biggest impact on fraud prevention over the next five years—AI, network analytics, real-time monitoring, or something else?

AI agent open loops can burn 2M tokens in a single run.

Closed loops fix that; here's the difference...

Most people use AI agents by prompting one task at a time. Build this. Now fix that. Now write the tests. 

You drive every step and that makes YOU the bottleneck.

Loop Engineering flips the model. You build the system that prompts the agent. It runs until the goal is met.

📌 The AI Agent Loop has 5 steps:

 1. Discovery → The agent finds what it needs before acting. No guessing. No missing context.

 2. Planning → It breaks the goal into clear, executable steps. Scope defined. Path set.

 3. Execution → The agent does the actual work writing, analyzing, building, connecting tools.

 4. Verification → It checks its output against the goal and your quality standard.

 5. Iteration → fixes the gaps and runs again until the work clears the bar.

📌 Every good loop has 6 building blocks:

 1. Automations → runs on a schedule, not on you 

 2. Worktrees → parallel agents, zero file collisions 

 3. Skills → project knowledge written once, read every loop 

 4. Plugins & Connectors → connects to PRs, tickets, Slack

 5. Subagents → maker and checker are never the same agent

 6. Memory → lives outside the conversation, never forgets

 → Single Agent vs Fleet: One agent improving its own work is enough for most tasks. A fleet of specialists is for when one brain isn't enough.

 → The Quality Gate: No gate = slop machine. Build it from things the agent can't argue with tests, linters, CI, type systems. Not a comment it can rationalize around.

 → The Self-Learning Loop: Agents forget between runs. Write lessons to a RULES.md file on disk. Every mistake caught becomes a wall forever. Your loop gets smarter every cycle.

📌 Where do you sit on the Loop Engineering Maturity scale?

Level 1 — Prompter: One task at a time. Reactive. 
Level 2 — Operator: Runs agents manually. Hands-on. 
Level 3 — Loop Engineer: Designs agent loops. Proactive. 
Level 4 — System Architect: Builds self-improving agent ecosystems. Strategic.

The teams winning with AI agents aren't just better prompters. They're loop engineers.

Most people think Generative AI started with ChatGPT.

It didn't.

ChatGPT is the product.

Generative Deep Learning is the foundation.

A few years ago, if you wanted a machine to generate something new a sentence, an image, a piece of music, or even code it felt almost impossible.

Traditional machine learning was great at classification.

Is this spam or not?

Will this customer churn?

What is the probability of fraud?

But Generative Deep Learning changed the question entirely.

Instead of predicting labels, models started learning patterns.

And once they learned patterns, they could create.
Create text.

Create images.

Create videos.

Create software.

That shift changed everything.

Today, when you ask an AI assistant to write an email, generate a marketing strategy, build a React application, or summarize a 200-page document, you're seeing years of progress in Generative Deep Learning at work.

But here's what fascinates me most:
The real value is no longer in generating content.
The real value is in generating outcomes.

We're moving from:
AI that writes → AI that works
AI that answers → AI that acts
AI that predicts → AI that executes

That's why concepts like:
• Transformers
• Attention Mechanisms
• Diffusion Models
• RAG
• AI Agents
• Reinforcement Learning

are becoming essential knowledge for modern engineers.

The next generation of software won't just be applications.

It will be intelligent systems that reason, retrieve information, use tools, make decisions, and complete tasks.

Generative Deep Learning is not another technology trend.

It's becoming the operating system of the AI era.

The engineers who understand how these systems work under the hood today will be building the companies everyone talks about tomorrow.

What concept in Generative Deep Learning had the biggest impact on your understanding of AI?

I fine-tuned a 7B medical LLM on a free GPU that beats the GPT-4 Model.

In just 9.5 hours. For Indian patients that every other AI ignores. Here's exactly what I did and what I learned.

Most clinical AI is built on Western data, tested on Western patients, and deployed in Western hospitals. Meanwhile 1.4 billion Indians are describing symptoms in Hinglish to chatbots that have never heard the word "bukhar."

So I fixed that.

The setup: 
→ Base model: BioMistral-7B (EPFL clinical LLM) 
→ Method: QLoRA (4-bit quantization) + Unsloth 2x speedup 
→ Data: Synthetic Indian medical Q&A records 
→ Hardware: Kaggle T4 GPU completely free 
→ Time: 9.5 hours, 873 steps, 5 epochs

The results: 
→ Train loss: 0.065 at final step 
→ Val loss: 0.163 (target was < 1.0 ✅) 
→ Adapter size: 167.8 MB LoRA safetensors 
→ Only 0.5% of parameters actually trained

What it can do that GPT-4 can't: 
→ Understands Indian medical terminology natively 
→ Responds in both English and Hindi in the same conversation 
→ Generates structured and classified treatment plans 
→ Plugs into an XGBoost risk analyzer scoring patients 0–100 with SHAP explainability 

The biggest lesson?
You don't need GPT-4 for every problem. You need the right data, a clear problem, and the discipline to fine-tune instead of prompt-engineer your way around a bad foundation model.

A 7B model trained on domain-specific data will beat a 175B general model on your specific task. Every time.


AI agents don't become powerful because they're given better prompts.
They become powerful because they're given better patterns.

A lot of agent workflows are just:
Prompt → Response

That works until the task requires planning, tool usage, self-correction, or collaboration.

That's where agentic patterns come in.
→ ReAct: Think, act, observe, repeat
→ Plan & Execute: Create a plan before taking action
→ Reflection: Review and improve your own output
→ Tool Use: Access APIs, databases, and external systems
→ Multi-Agent: Divide work across specialized agents

The interesting part?
Most advanced AI systems today are combinations of these patterns.
Not bigger prompts.
Not another framework.
Patterns.

A useful mental model:
LLMs provide intelligence.
Agentic patterns provide structure.
And structure is what turns a capable model into a reliable system.

Before building an agent, ask:
Does it need to think?
Does it need to plan?
Does it need tools?
Does it need to collaborate?
The answers usually tell you which pattern to use.

Most people still think AI gets its facts from Google.
That is only partly true.
A Semrush study of 150,000 LLM citations
shows a much broader pattern.

The most-cited source was reddit .com at 40.1%.

Google was 23.3%.
Then came Yelp, Facebook, Amazon, Tripadvisor,
Mapbox, OpenStreetMap, Instagram, and others.

That list matters because it changes the visibility game.
AI systems are not only summarising expert pages.
They are leaning on what is public,
structured,
widely discussed,
and easy to reference.

For leaders, this is a practical warning.
If your company only thinks about classic SEO,
you may be missing where AI systems actually look.
Your brand, product, and category language
may be shaped by conversations and databases
outside your own website.

That includes community discussions.
It includes public profiles.
It includes local and structured data sources.
It includes video platforms and marketplaces.
It includes places where customers describe the problem
in their own words.

This is why AI visibility is not just a marketing channel.
It is an information quality problem.
If the public record is messy,
outdated,
or dominated by third-party assumptions,
AI answers may reflect that mess back to buyers.

The response is not to spam every platform.
The response is to make your useful proof easier to find.
Clear product pages.
Clear category explanations.
Accurate listings.
Useful public answers.
Documented customer language.
Consistent facts across the web.

Think of it as reputation infrastructure.
Search used to reward discoverability.
AI rewards retrievability and citation-worthiness.
That means the best source is not always the loudest source.
It is the source the model can find,
trust enough to quote,
and connect to the question being asked.

The shift is already visible.
AI results are built from public signals,
not just private expertise.

For executives, this turns content into infrastructure.
Every public explanation becomes training context
for future buyer questions.

The question for every brand is simple:
when AI looks for facts about your market,
what public evidence is it going to find?

Vector databases are no longer the default answer for every AI agent.

For years, most teams thought:

RAG = chunks + embeddings + vector database

But that assumption is now being challenged.

Traditional vector RAG works like this:

You take a large document.

Break it into chunks.

Convert those chunks into embeddings.

Retrieve based on similarity.

That works well for simple Q&A.

But with complex documents like financial filings, legal contracts, compliance reports, or technical manuals, similarity is not always enough.

The system may find what sounds relevant.

But not always what is actually relevant.

That is where Vectorless RAG becomes interesting.

Instead of only relying on chunks and embeddings, Vectorless RAG reads the document structurally.

It understands:

📌 Chapters
📌 Sections
📌 Subsections
📌 Table of contents
📌 Page-level references

Retrieval becomes more reasoning-based instead of just similarity-based.

Here’s the simple flow:

1️⃣ Document Indexing
The document is parsed into a hierarchical tree structure.
No traditional chunking, no embeddings, and no vector DB required.

2️⃣ Tree-Based Reasoning
The LLM moves through the document structure from high-level sections to the exact relevant part.

3️⃣ Context-Aware Retrieval
It uses conversation history and document structure to return traceable answers with page and section references.

4️⃣ Agentic Execution
AI agents can search, reason, validate, and answer using the document structure directly.

Why does this matter?

Because the RAG conversation is shifting.

It is no longer only about:

“Which vector database should we use?”

The better question is:

“Do we need vectors for this use case at all?”

Vector databases are still powerful.

But for complex professional documents, structure-aware retrieval may give better accuracy, better traceability, and more reliable answers.

The future of RAG may not be vector-only.

It may be hybrid, reasoning-based, and document-aware.

Your RAG pipeline can fail even when your embeddings are good.
Because retrieval problems often start much earlier.
Chunking.

Most people still do:
→ 500 tokens
→ 50 overlap
→ Push to vector DB
→ Hope retrieval works
That works.

Until production data shows up.
Messy PDFs.
Multi topic documents.
Broken OCR.
Structured manuals.
Mixed enterprise content.
Suddenly…
Your chunks are splitting meaning.
Context becomes noisy.
Retrieval precision drops.

The model is blamed.
The chunking strategy was the bottleneck.

In this carousel, I break down 5 chunking approaches used in real RAG systems:
→ Fixed / Sliding Window Chunking
→ Semantic Chunking
→ Hierarchical Chunking
→ LLM Based Chunking
→ Agentic Chunking

Different data.
Different tradeoffs.
Different failure modes.

Not every use case needs advanced chunking.
But choosing the wrong chunking strategy can quietly destroy your retrieval quality.

Production RAG is not:
"One chunking strategy for everything."
It's:
"Choose chunking based on data, retrieval goals, cost, and document structure."


Five AI tools. Five completely different strengths.
Here's which one to open and when.


GEMINI: Open it when you need to work with large amounts of data.

Analysis of massive documents, searching across video and image libraries, full Google Workspace integration.
If your workflow lives in Google Docs, Sheets, or Drive, Gemini reads and works inside those tools natively. No copy-pasting.

CHATGPT: Open it when you need a broad general assistant with a large tool ecosystem.

Voice and video interaction, a vast library of custom GPTs and agent tools, strong logical reasoning for everyday tasks.
If you need a tool that does a little of everything and has the widest ecosystem of add-ons, this is the one.

CLAUDE: Open it when the output needs to sound human and be technically precise.

Superior coding and technical reasoning, natural tone for high-stakes documents, writing that doesn't sound like AI wrote it.
If you're drafting something that represents your brand, a proposal, a client email, a content piece, Claude is built for nuance in a way the others aren't.

PERPLEXITY: Open it when you need live, sourced information.
Instant answers from real-time web results, mandatory source citations, multi-threaded research.

If you need current facts with verifiable sources, not training data from months ago, this is the right tool. Not a replacement for deep work, but the best starting point for research.

GROK: Open it when you need real-time social and trend intelligence.
Exclusive access to X real-time data, aggressive reasoning and math capabilities, fast responses on what's happening right now.

If you're tracking trends, monitoring conversations, or need to know what's being said online at this moment, Grok has access no other model does.

——

The mistake most people make is picking one and using it for everything.

These tools aren't competing for the same job. They're built for different ones.

Gemini for data. ChatGPT for breadth. Claude for quality. Perplexity for facts. Grok for now.

Which one are you underusing? ↓


🚀 SKILL.md Might Be the Most Important AI Standard You Haven’t Heard About Yet

While everyone is talking about AI agents, a quiet revolution is happening behind the scenes: SKILL.md.

Think of it as a universal app store format for AI capabilities—allowing agents to discover, load, and execute specialized skills on demand.

🔹 Why it matters

One standard, multiple AI platforms

Reusable agent capabilities

Reduced context window bloat

Better maintainability and scalability

Vendor-neutral approach


📌 What makes SKILL.md powerful? ✅ Progressive loading of instructions
✅ Portable across Claude, OpenAI, Gemini, Cursor, GitHub Copilot, VS Code, and more
✅ Supports executable scripts, references, and documentation
✅ Encourages modular AI engineering practices

The Shift We're Seeing

Before:

Massive prompts

Repeated instructions

Context overload

Hard-to-maintain agent workflows


After:

Modular skills

On-demand expertise

Cleaner agent architecture

Reusable workflows across tools


Skills vs Configs vs MCP

Skills = What the agent knows how to do

Configs = How the agent behaves

MCP = External tools and systems it can access


Together, they form the foundation of modern Agentic AI systems.

The future of AI engineering won't be built with larger prompts—it will be built with composable skills, tool orchestration, and standardized interfaces.

Most AI engineers in 2026 have never built the thing they ship every day.
We fine-tune. We orchestrate. We chain. We deploy.

But almost none of us have written the actual loop that makes a model think.

Someone open-sourced the fix. Free. MIT. One GPU.

𝐭𝐫𝐚𝐢𝐧-𝐥𝐥𝐦-𝐟𝐫𝐨𝐦-𝐬𝐜𝐫𝐚𝐭𝐜𝐡 takes you from an empty file to an aligned reasoning model. No Hugging Face transformers. No trl. No peft. Every line is yours.

The full chain, hand-written:
1️⃣ Build a Transformer from raw PyTorch
2️⃣ Pre-train on real text (the Pile)
3️⃣ SFT, teach it to follow instructions
4️⃣ Reward Model, score good vs bad answers
5️⃣ PPO / DPO, let it improve its own output
6️⃣ GRPO, teach it to reason (DeepSeek-R1 style)

Real datasets the whole way: Alpaca, Dolly, Anthropic HH-RLHF, UltraFeedback, GSM8K.

The author trained a 13M model on a single RTX 5090 using ~0.6 GB of VRAM. The barrier was never compute. It was never the math. It was that the entire stack got abstracted away before most of us ever saw it.

We optimized for shipping fast. We traded understanding for velocity.

The gap in 2026 isn't between people who use AI and people who don't. It's between people who can call a model and people who know what happens when they do.

One of those two is about to get a lot more valuable.





gnani.ai, a Bengaluru-based voice AI startup, has launched its latest speech-to-text model Prisma v2.5, designed specifically to handle India’s complex and diverse speech patterns, including rural accents and code-mixed languages.

The company claims that the new model delivers higher accuracy than competing systems such as ElevenLabs, Deepgram, and Sarvam AI on Indian speech benchmarks. 

According to its internal evaluation, Prisma v2.5 ranks #1 in 8 out of 9 Indian languages, especially in real-world and noisy audio environments.

A key focus of the model is improving performance on code-mixed speech, where speakers naturally blend languages like Hindi-English or Tamil-English in the same sentence. 

This is a major challenge for global AI systems, which are usually trained on single-language datasets and struggle with India’s multilingual communication style.

Gnani.ai also reported performance improvements such as a 15% reduction in word error rate for rural Hindi dialects and an 18% improvement across Dravidian languages, highlighting its focus on regional and underrepresented speech patterns

This is probably the most honest AI architecture breakdown on the internet right now.

9-layer AI production architecture :

→ services/ - RAG pipeline, semantic cache, memory, query rewriter, router. Not one file. Five.

→ agents/ - document grader, decomposer, adaptive router. Self-correcting by design.

→ prompts/ - versioned, typed, registered. Never hardcoded.

→ security/ - input, content, output. Three guards, not one.

→ evaluation/ - golden dataset, offline eval, online monitor. Most people skip this entire layer and ship blind.

→ observability/ - per-stage tracing, feedback linked to traces, cost per query.

→ .claude/ - agent context so your AI coding assistant knows the codebase before it touches a file

Claude Code’s architecture is one of the clearest public examples of what modern AI Agent systems actually look like.

After studying the architecture, one thing stood out to me:

The model is not the system.

Most discussions around AI focus on model capabilities, but Claude Code shows that the real value emerges from the layers around the model:

• Permission & Governance  
• Memory & Context Management  
• Tool Dispatch & MCP Runtime  
• Multi-Agent Coordination  
• Observability & Auditability

At the center sits a surprisingly simple Master Agent Loop.

The loop itself is intentionally "dumb".

The intelligence comes from the surrounding orchestration layers.

This is why I increasingly think about AI systems in two major domains:

🔹 Harness
The control plane responsible for governance, memory, tool orchestration, agent coordination, and observation.

🔹 Skills
The reusable capabilities that agents can discover, compose, execute, and improve over time.

In our diagrams:
Harness = The Orchestrator  
InsForge = The Skill System

Claude Code is a great case study showing that the future of AI architecture may be less about building larger models, and more about building better systems around them.

Vector Databases in AI.

Traditional databases are great for exact searches. But AI applications often need to search by meaning. That is where vector databases come in.

Documents are converted into embeddings (vectors) and stored in a vector database.

When a user asks a question:

Question → Embedding → Similarity Search → Retrieved Context → LLM Answer
This is the foundation of RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) systems.

Popular vector databases include:

FAISS
Chroma
Pinecone
Weaviate
Qdrant
pgvector

One key lesson:

Vector databases do not replace SQL databases.
They complement them by enabling semantic search.
Understanding vector databases is essential for building production AI applications such as chatbots, document search systems, and AI assistants.


Your RAG system is giving wrong answers.
The first instinct is usually:
"Let's improve the prompt."
That's often the wrong fix.

Because most production AI failures are not prompt problems.
They're visibility problems.
You can't fix what you can't see.

When a RAG system fails, the issue could be anywhere:
→ Query rewriting
→ Embeddings
→ Retrieval
→ Reranking
→ Context construction
→ Tool calls
→ Model generation

Yet many teams treat the entire pipeline like a black box.
User asks a question.
Model gives a bad answer.
Everyone blames the LLM.
Production AI doesn't work that way.

The best AI teams spend as much time on observability as they do on prompts.

Because observability answers the only question that matters:
"Where exactly did the system break?"

That's why every serious AI application needs 4 things:
Traces
See the complete journey of every request.

Metrics
Track latency, retrieval quality, token usage, hallucinations, and failures.

Logs
Capture infrastructure problems before they become user problems.

Evaluation & Feedback
Measure whether the system is actually improving over time.

Without observability:
→ You guess
→ You tweak prompts randomly
→ You over-engineer solutions
→ You waste weeks chasing the wrong problem

With observability:
→ You know why retrieval failed
→ You know which tool returned bad data
→ You know which chunk was dropped
→ You know exactly what to fix
Production AI is not about fancy prompts.
It's about visibility.

Because the engineers who can debug AI systems are far more valuable than the engineers who can only build demos.


🚀 CRAG = CAG + RAG: The Next Evolution of Enterprise Retrieval Systems

As organizations scale AI applications into production, a single retrieval strategy is often not enough.

Traditional RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) excels at accessing fresh, dynamic information, but repeatedly retrieving static enterprise knowledge can increase latency, cost, and token consumption.

This is where CRAG (Cache-Augmented Retrieval-Augmented Generation) becomes a powerful architectural pattern:

🔹 CAG (Cache-Augmented Generation) – Cold Knowledge

Preload stable information into cache:

✅ Policies & procedures
✅ Product documentation
✅ Technical manuals
✅ Compliance frameworks
✅ Knowledge base articles

Benefits:

Lower latency

Reduced vector searches

Lower token usage

Predictable performance



---

🔹 RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) – Hot Knowledge

Retrieve information dynamically when needed:

✅ Real-time databases
✅ APIs & business systems
✅ Market data
✅ Operational dashboards
✅ Frequently changing content

Benefits:

Up-to-date responses

Context-aware answers

Access to live enterprise data



---

🔹 CRAG Architecture Flow

1️⃣ User submits a query
2️⃣ Cached enterprise knowledge (CAG) is loaded instantly
3️⃣ System prompt and user query are combined
4️⃣ Dynamic retrieval (RAG) fetches relevant live information
5️⃣ Both contexts are merged into the LLM context window
6️⃣ LLM generates a grounded response


---

Why CRAG Matters

Challenge	CRAG Advantage

High latency	Faster responses through caching
Expensive retrieval	Reduced vector search frequency
Stale knowledge	Dynamic retrieval for freshness
Large context requirements	Efficient context composition
Enterprise scale	Improved cost-performance ratio

---

Enterprise Use Cases

🔹 Customer Support Agents
🔹 Internal Knowledge Assistants
🔹 Banking & Insurance Copilots
🔹 Manufacturing Operations AI
🔹 Healthcare Knowledge Systems
🔹 Software Engineering Assistants

• Vibe Coding & AI Automation – Build faster with AI copilots. Replace manual workflows while keeping processes safe, compliant, and cost-controlled.

• AI Observability & Production Scaling – Detect failures before users do. Monitor quality, latency, hallucinations, and drift while running AI at enterprise scale.

The common thread: speed, reliability, and value creation. Those who master these skills won’t just use AI-they’ll lead with it.

Non-coders are quietly building better AI systems than developers.
Why?
They focus on workflows, outcomes, and automation, not syntax.
If you want to see how this works in real business use cases
👉 Comment/DM AI

Everyone talks about LLMs.
But building production-ready Voice AI is a completely different game.

Especially in India.

Latency, telephony reliability, multilingual support, and real-time interruptions matter much more than just “which model is smartest.”

I mapped some of the most relevant Voice AI players and infrastructure layers startups are using right now:

→ Bolna (YC F25) — fast-growing India-first voice infra
→ gnani.ai — strong enterprise voice stack
→ Retell AI — great for low-latency AI calling
→ Vapi — developer-friendly voice orchestration
→ Telnyx — strong carrier-grade telephony layer
→ Yellow.ai / Verloop — CX automation at scale
→ Sarvam AI — pushing hard on Bharat-first language intelligence

My view:

If you’re building in India, your stack should be chosen based on:

✅ Language depth (Hindi, Telugu, Tamil, Kannada, etc.)
✅ Call latency & interruption handling
✅ Carrier integrations
✅ Cost at scale
✅ Customizability

A strong stack today could look like:

STT/TTS + Telephony + LLM + Workflow Engine

For example:
Sarvam / Bolna + Vapi / Retell + Telnyx + OpenAI/Groq + FreeSWITCH

If you are interviewing for an AI PM role this year, you will get most of these 8 questions.

Same 8. Across FAANG, startups, and consulting firms.

The candidates who land the offer don't memorize answers. They have a clear framework for each one.

Here are the 8 questions you will be asked, with the framework to answer 👇

𝟏. "𝐃𝐞𝐬𝐢𝐠𝐧 𝐚𝐧 𝐀𝐈 𝐟𝐞𝐚𝐭𝐮𝐫𝐞 𝐟𝐨𝐫 [𝐩𝐫𝐨𝐝𝐮𝐜𝐭]"
→ Framework: Problem → Users → Task → Eval → UX → Guardrails → Rollout
↳ The classic. Use the AI PRD template structure.

𝟐. "𝐖𝐨𝐮𝐥𝐝 𝐲𝐨𝐮 𝐔𝐬𝐞 𝐑𝐀𝐆, 𝐟𝐢𝐧𝐞-𝐭𝐮𝐧𝐞, 𝐨𝐫 𝐩𝐫𝐨𝐦𝐩𝐭?"
→ Framework: Cheapest first. Prompt → RAG → fine-tune.
→ RAG for knowledge, prompt for behavior, fine-tune for style.
↳ The wrong answer is "we fine-tune."

𝟑. "𝐇𝐨𝐰 𝐰𝐨𝐮𝐥𝐝 𝐲𝐨𝐮 𝐦𝐞𝐚𝐬𝐮𝐫𝐞 𝐭𝐡𝐢𝐬 𝐀𝐈 𝐟𝐞𝐚𝐭𝐮𝐫𝐞?"
→ Framework: Faithfulness + Relevance + Latency + Cost + Safety + Satisfaction
↳ Reference the 6-metric framework. Tie each to a failure mode.

𝟒. "𝐖𝐡𝐚𝐭 𝐢𝐟 𝐢𝐭 𝐡𝐚𝐥𝐥𝐮𝐜𝐢𝐧𝐚𝐭𝐞𝐬?"
→ Framework: Grounding + Citations + Confidence + Refusal + Human-in-loop
↳ The interviewer wants to hear you respect the failure mode.

𝟓. "𝐇𝐨𝐰 𝐰𝐨𝐮𝐥𝐝 𝐲𝐨𝐮 𝐩𝐫𝐢𝐨𝐫𝐢𝐭𝐢𝐳𝐞 𝐀𝐈 𝐢𝐧𝐯𝐞𝐬𝐭𝐦𝐞𝐧𝐭𝐬?"
→ Framework: Impact vs Effort vs AI-fit (does AI actually help?)
↳ Many problems do not need AI. Saying that wins points.

𝟔. "𝐓𝐞𝐥𝐥 𝐦𝐞 𝐚𝐛𝐨𝐮𝐭 𝐚𝐧 𝐀𝐈 𝐟𝐞𝐚𝐭𝐮𝐫𝐞 𝐲𝐨𝐮 𝐬𝐡𝐢𝐩𝐩𝐞𝐝"
→ Framework: STAR (Situation/Task/Action/Result)
→ Lead with the eval results and the business outcome
↳ "We shipped to 100% with 0 incidents" beats any technical detail.

𝟕. "𝐇𝐨𝐰 𝐝𝐨 𝐲𝐨𝐮 𝐰𝐨𝐫𝐤 𝐰𝐢𝐭𝐡 𝐌𝐋 𝐞𝐧𝐠𝐢𝐧𝐞𝐞𝐫𝐬?"
→ Framework: Shared eval set + clear acceptance criteria + co-owned metrics
↳ "I let MLEs decide model choice" is the wrong answer. So is the opposite.

𝟖. "𝐖𝐡𝐚𝐭 𝐤𝐞𝐞𝐩𝐬 𝐲𝐨𝐮 𝐮𝐩 𝐚𝐭 𝐧𝐢𝐠𝐡𝐭 𝐚𝐛𝐨𝐮𝐭 𝐀𝐈?"
→ Framework: Pick one risk you genuinely care about (drift, bias, cost, misuse)
→ Show you have thought about it deeply
↳ "Nothing" disqualifies you.

The candidates who get the offer:

→ Reference frameworks (not opinions)
→ Cite specific projects with eval numbers
→ Show respect for AI failure modes
→ Have one honest weakness they are working on

What other AI PM question have you been asked? 👇

AUTOMATION    vs    AGENTIC AI
🚨 "Why are we using LangGraph? Can't LangChain do all of this?"

A client asked me this during an AI Automation workshop.
My immediate thought?
💭 "Probably."

At that point, the requirements looked straightforward.
🔧 The AI needed to:
✅ Connect to multiple business systems
✅ Retrieve information from various sources
✅ Execute actions through an MCP Server
✅ Generate responses for users

This is exactly where LangChain shines.
Simple. Structured. Predictable.

But then the conversation took an interesting turn.
The client asked:
❓ "What happens if the data is incomplete?"
❓ "Can it verify the answer before taking action?"
❓ "What if one system doesn't have the information?"
❓ "Can it ask another agent to investigate?"
❓ "Can it decide the next step on its own?"
⚡ Suddenly, we weren't discussing automation anymore.

We were discussing decision-making.
And that's where many AI projects quietly cross a very important line.

Most teams believe they've built an AI Agent because the AI can call a tool.
But here's the reality:
🔥 Tool calling isn't intelligence. Decision-making is.
🏗️ The Architectural Difference

🔹 Traditional AI Automation
Input → Tool → Result → Output
✔ The path is predefined
✔ The workflow is predictable
✔ The AI follows instructions

🔹 Agentic AI
Input
   ↓
Analyze
   ↓
Decide
   ↓
Execute
   ↓
Evaluate
   ↓
Retry / Redirect
   ↓
Output
✔ The path is dynamic
✔ The AI adapts based on outcomes
✔ The system can change strategy mid-process

🧩 How We Solved It
Instead of replacing one framework with another, we used both where they were strongest.

⚙️ LangChain
✔ MCP integrations
✔ Tool orchestration
✔ Data access workflows
✔ External system connectivity

🧠 LangGraph
✔ Decision-making
✔ Agent coordination
✔ Dynamic workflow routing
✔ Error recovery & replanning
✔ Multi-step reasoning
🎯 The Outcome

What we built wasn't just a smarter MCP Server.
It became a system that could:
🔍 Assess what information was missing
🔄 Change its approach when a path failed
🤝 Delegate work to specialized agents
📊 Evaluate results before taking action
🎯 Adapt its strategy based on what happened in previous steps

💡 My Takeaway
The transition from AI Automation to Agentic AI doesn't happen when an AI can use a tool.

It happens when the AI can:
👉 Decide which tool to use
👉 Decide when to use it
👉 Decide what to do next when things don't go as expected

That's the difference between:
📋 Following a process
and
🧠 Reasoning through a problem

❓ How are you drawing the line between AI Automation and Agentic AI in your projects?

I'm curious how other architects, CTOs, and AI leaders are approaching this challenge.

👇 Share your thoughts in the comments.
Share this with your network so others can stay aware of these fundamentals.

𝐒𝐭𝐨𝐩 𝐁𝐮𝐢𝐥𝐝𝐢𝐧𝐠 𝐁𝐚𝐬𝐢𝐜 𝐀𝐩𝐩𝐬: 𝐓𝐡𝐞 𝐁𝐥𝐮𝐞𝐩𝐫𝐢𝐧𝐭 𝐟𝐨𝐫 𝐒𝐜𝐚𝐥𝐚𝐛𝐥𝐞 𝐀𝐈 𝐀𝐫𝐜𝐡𝐢𝐭𝐞𝐜𝐭𝐮𝐫𝐞 𝐨𝐧 𝐀𝐳𝐮𝐫𝐞

Artificial Intelligence (AI) has evolved from simple data analysis to a sophisticated technology capable of imitating human behavior through speech synthesis, natural interaction, and content generation. 

The attached document from Microsoft Learn, outlines the fundamental architectural components of the Azure AI landscape, spanning from traditional Machine Learning (ML) to the latest Generative AI (GenAI) frameworks. 

Some of the concepts covered:

Algorithms: Step-by-step instructions used to discover patterns and categorize information (e.g., identifying languages or types of objects).

Machine Learning (ML): A technique that uses algorithms to create predictive models. These models learn from past observations through a process called "training" and improve over time via retraining.

Deep Learning: A subset of ML utilizing artificial neural networks with multiple processing layers. While capable of creating more complex models, it requires a significant investment for customization.

Generative AI: Trained to create original content (text, image, audio, or code) from everyday language inputs.

Language Models: A subset of GenAI focused on natural language processing (NLP).

Large Language Models (LLMs): Models with more than 10 billion parameters.

Small Language Models (SLMs): Models with fewer than 10 billion parameters. These are often more efficient and interpretable.

Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG): RAG is an architecture pattern that scopes generative AI to specific, trusted content. It adds a retrieval system to a language model to provide "grounding data" from vectorized documents or images.

Agent-Based Architecture: Agents are autonomous entities that do more than respond to prompts; they make decisions and interact with other systems.

Multi-agent architecture: Breaks complex problems into specialized agents that coordinate solutions.

Foundry Agent Service: Provides a platform to host these agents, connecting them to foundation models and private knowledge stores.

Fine-Tuning: Tailoring models to personal datasets to achieve higher quality results with shorter prompts and lower latency.

Automated Machine Learning (AutoML): Automates iterative tasks like model selection and hyperparameter tuning, allowing developers without deep data science expertise to build high-quality models.

Specialized Tools:

Custom Speech: Improves recognition for domain-specific vocabulary and unique audio conditions.

Custom Translator: Allows enterprises to build neural machine translation systems using their own industry terminology.

Azure Document Intelligence: Uses ML to extract structured data from forms and documents.

Azure Content Understanding: Uses GenAI to ingest and process documents, images, videos, and audio into user-defined formats.


Dear LinkedIn family,

 Future of Agentic AI is Being Standardised — Enter MCP Architecture 
The shift in AI is no longer about better models. It’s about systems that can think, decide, and act autonomously. 
Welcome to the era of Agentic AI 👇 

⚠️ The Challenge We Faced

Until recently, building agentic systems meant dealing with: 
Fragmented tool integrations
Inconsistent context handling
Vendor-locked frameworks 
👉 Result: Complex, brittle, and hard-to-scale architectures 

✅ The Shift: Model Context Protocol (MCP)

A major breakthrough is emerging: MCP — the universal connectivity layer for AI agents 
👉 Think of it as: “USB-C for AI systems” 
It standardises how agents: 
Access tools
Retrieve data
Execute actions
Share context 
🧠 MCP Architecture (Simplified)

1. Host Layer 
AI Apps (Copilot, IDEs, enterprise platforms) 
2. Agent Layer 
Reasoning, planning, orchestration 
3. MCP Layer (Connector Fabric) 
Unified access to APIs, DBs, SaaS, workflows 
👉 Agents focus on what to do 👉 MCP handles how to execute 

🔁 From GenAI → Agentic Systems
Traditional AI
Agentic AI + MCP
Prompt-driven
Goal-driven
Static responses
Action execution
Custom integrations
Standard protocol
Single agent
Multi-agent systems

🤖 Multi-Agent Future

With MCP + emerging A2A protocols: 
Agents collaborate like teams
Tasks are decomposed dynamically
Workflows run in parallel 
Example: 
Planner → breaks tasks
Researcher → gathers data
Executor → performs actions
Validator → ensures compliance 
🏗️ What This Means for Architects

✔️ Interoperability by design ✔️ Plug-and-play enterprise integrations ✔️ Traceable, governed AI workflows ✔️ Scalable multi-agent ecosystems 

🌍 Industry Direction

Major players are converging toward: 
MCP (tool & data standardisation)
A2A (agent collaboration)
Open agent ecosystems 
👉 This is the HTTP moment for AI agents 

🔮 Final Thought

The future of AI won’t be a single powerful model… It will be an ecosystem of specialised agents connected through standard protocols.

MCP is becoming the foundation of that future.

𝐄𝐯𝐞𝐫𝐲𝐨𝐧𝐞'𝐬 𝐭𝐡𝐫𝐨𝐰𝐢𝐧𝐠 𝐚𝐫𝐨𝐮𝐧𝐝 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐬𝐚𝐦𝐞 𝟏𝟐 𝐰𝐨𝐫𝐝𝐬 𝐫𝐢𝐠𝐡𝐭 𝐧𝐨𝐰.
RAG. Agent. Agentic AI. MCP. Tools. Skills. Hooks. Subagents. Memory. Context. Orchestration. Eval.

Master these 12 terms and watch the whole game change. You stop guessing why your agent breaks and start seeing exactly which layer failed.

Each one sits at a different layer, and the whole thing only works when they stack.

So I drew the entire stack out — one picture per idea, plain English, no jargon 👇

𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗳𝗼𝘂𝗻𝗱𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻
→ 𝗥𝗔𝗚 — pulls in outside knowledge before answering
→ 𝗖𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗲𝘅𝘁 — everything the model can see right now
→ 𝗠𝗲𝗺𝗼𝗿𝘆 — what it carries across turns and sessions

𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗮𝗴𝗲𝗻𝘁 𝗶𝘁𝘀𝗲𝗹𝗳
→ 𝗔𝗴𝗲𝗻𝘁 — an AI that acts, not just answers
→ 𝗔𝗴𝗲𝗻𝘁𝗶𝗰 𝗔𝗜 — systems that plan, decide, and self-correct
→ 𝗧𝗼𝗼𝗹𝘀 — external functions the agent can call
→ 𝗠𝗖𝗣 — one open standard to connect tools and data

𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗰𝗮𝗽𝗮𝗯𝗶𝗹𝗶𝘁𝗶𝗲𝘀
→ 𝗦𝗸𝗶𝗹𝗹𝘀 — reusable know-how, loaded on demand
→ 𝗛𝗼𝗼𝗸𝘀 — triggers that run logic at set points
→ 𝗦𝘂𝗯𝗮𝗴𝗲𝗻𝘁𝘀 — specialist helpers the main agent hands work to

𝗥𝘂𝗻𝗻𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝘀𝘆𝘀𝘁𝗲𝗺
→ 𝗢𝗿𝗰𝗵𝗲𝘀𝘁𝗿𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻 — coordinates the whole multi-step workflow
→ 𝗘𝘃𝗮𝗹 — measures whether any of it actually works

Here's the part most people skip:

The foundation layer decides what your agent knows.
The agent layer decides what it can do.
The capability layer decides how well it scales.
And Eval is the only layer that tells you the truth about the other three.

You can have perfect tools and brittle memory and still ship something that fails in production. The pieces don't compete - they compose. Get one wrong and the whole stack feels broken.

The teams winning with agents right now aren't the ones who picked the best single primitive. They're the ones who understand how all twelve fit together.

If the field standardized on one shared vocabulary for this stack, which of these 12 terms do you think is still the most misunderstood?


Think of an AI system like a human body

Most AI projects don’t fail because the model is weak.
They fail because the architecture is overcomplicated from day one.

A simpler way to scope modern AI systems:

• Need language understanding, summarization, classification, or reasoning?
→ Start with an LLM.

• Need answers grounded in company docs or changing knowledge?
→ Add RAG.

• Need secure access to tools, APIs, databases, or workflows?
→ Use MCP or direct integrations.

• Need multi-step decision making and autonomous execution?
→ That’s when AI Agents make sense.

The mistake:
Many teams jump directly into “agentic AI” before defining what actually needs intelligence, retrieval, or actions.

Good AI architecture is layered.
Not everything needs an agent.

The 5 Layers of Enterprise Agentic AI

🤖 Enterprise Agentic AI as a 5-layer architecture 👩💻 :

🚨 As enterprises move from AI assistants to AI agents, a common question emerges:

👉 What does a production-ready Agentic AI architecture actually look like?

While Foundation Models provide the intelligence, enterprise-grade AI systems typically require multiple layers working together to deliver real business value.
A practical way to think about it is through these 5 layers:

Enterprise Agentic AI is much more than a foundation model.
Think of it as a 5-layer architecture:

🔹 Layer 1: Foundation Model
The reasoning engine.
Examples:
• GPT
• Claude
• Gemini
• Llama
This layer generates responses, but by itself it has no knowledge of your business, no memory, and no ability to take actions.
⬇️

🔹 Layer 2: RAG (Knowledge Layer)
Gives the model access to enterprise knowledge.
Examples:
• Policies
• SOPs
• Product documentation
• Customer contracts
Without RAG, your agent knows the world.
With RAG, it knows your business.
⬇️

🔹 Layer 3: Memory Layer
Allows the agent to remember context across interactions.
Examples:
• User preferences
• Previous conversations
• Long-running tasks
• Historical decisions
Without memory, every conversation starts from zero.
⬇️

🔹 Layer 4: Tool Layer (MCP)
Allows the agent to interact with enterprise systems.
Examples:
• CRM
• ERP
• Ticketing systems
• Databases
• Internal APIs
This is where AI moves from answering questions to getting work done.
⬇️

🔹 Layer 5: Agent Orchestration Layer
The brain that plans, reasons, delegates, and executes.
Responsibilities:
• Task decomposition
• Multi-step reasoning
• Tool selection
• Workflow execution
• Human approvals
This layer transforms AI from a chatbot into a digital worker.

💡 The biggest misconception in Enterprise AI today:
Many organizations stop at Layer 1.
The most successful implementations invest in Layers 2–5.
Because Agentic AI is not a model.
It's an architecture.

What's the biggest gap you see in enterprise AI implementations today: Knowledge, Memory, Tools, or Orchestration?

If you remove tool calling, memory, planning, and reflection...
What's left?
Just an LLM.
That's the part many people miss about AI agents.

They think agents are a new model.
A new framework.
A new technology.

They're not.
An AI agent is simply an LLM surrounded by components that help it think, act, and improve.
That's why understanding agent architecture matters more than learning agent frameworks.

Because every agent is built from a small set of building blocks.

Tool Calling
Gives the agent access to the outside world.
Search.
Databases.
APIs.
Code execution.
Without tools, agents can only generate text.

Function Calling
Turns model outputs into structured actions your application can execute reliably.
Planner
Breaks large goals into smaller tasks.
Research.
Analyze.
Execute.
Review.
Reflection

Allows the agent to critique its own work before delivering the final answer.
Memory
Stores information across steps and sessions.
Without memory, every interaction starts from zero.

Multi-Agent Systems
Specialized agents collaborate on a shared objective.
One researches.
One writes.
One reviews.
ReAct Loop
The pattern behind most modern agents:
Think
→ Act
→ Observe
→ Repeat

Here's the important part:
Production agents rarely use just one of these.
A real-world customer support agent might use:
→ Tool Calling
→ Memory
→ ReAct
→ Reflection

A research assistant might use:
→ Planner
→ Multi-Agent
→ Memory
→ Tool Calling

The architecture changes based on the problem.
Not the framework.

This is also why AI interviews are getting harder.

Companies no longer ask:
"What is an AI Agent?"
They ask:
"When would you use memory?"
"When do you need a planner?"
"Why use multi-agent instead of a single agent?"
"How would you debug a ReAct loop?"

That's where real understanding starts.
Frameworks will change.
Agent architectures will evolve.
But these building blocks will remain.
Learn the components.
The frameworks become easy.

Are APIs enough for the age of AI agents?

For decades, APIs have been the foundation of modern software, enabling applications to communicate and exchange data efficiently.But AI agents are fundamentally different from traditional applications.They don't just request information.They reason, plan, select tools, execute workflows, and interact with multiple systems to complete tasks.

That's why Model Context Protocol (MCP) is becoming one of the most important developments in the agentic AI ecosystem.

🔸 MCP vs Traditional APIs: What's Really Different?

🟡 Communication
• Traditional APIs rely on request-response interactions over HTTP.
• MCP enables richer, bidirectional communication between AI systems and external resources.

🟡 Purpose
• APIs were designed for web and mobile applications.
• MCP is designed for AI agents, copilots, IDEs, and intelligent systems that require dynamic tool access.

🟡 Context
• APIs typically return predefined responses.
• MCP allows agents to retrieve contextual information from databases, documents, file systems, and enterprise applications.

🟡 Flexibility
• APIs operate within fixed workflows.
• MCP allows agents to discover capabilities, select tools, and adapt their execution path dynamically.

🟡 Tool Access
• APIs expose endpoints.
• MCP exposes capabilities that AI systems can understand and utilize autonomously.

🟡 Architecture
• Traditional integrations often require custom connectors and orchestration layers.
• MCP introduces a standardized protocol layer designed specifically for AI interactions.

🟡 Business Impact
• APIs transformed application development.
• MCP is accelerating the development of agents, copilots, autonomous workflows, and intelligent enterprise systems.

💡 A useful way to think about it:

APIs connect applications. MCP connects intelligence to action.
As organizations move beyond chatbots and toward autonomous agents, success will depend on more than model quality. It will depend on how effectively AI can access context, use tools, and operate across enterprise systems.

If APIs were the backbone of the application era, MCP is emerging as a foundational layer for the agent era. The future of AI isn't just about generating answers.It's about getting work done.

What's your perspective on MCP's role in enterprise AI architectures?

LangChain is the bridge between LLMs and real-world applications.
 -> Combines LLMs with memory, tools, and data
 -> Enables multi-step workflows & agentic reasoning
 -> Powers use cases like RAG, chatbots, and AI assistants
Think of it this way:
 LLM = Brain 
 LangChain = Nervous system connecting it to the world

🚀 Interview Insight: Beyond RAG – Understanding KAG & CAG

Today, during an AI/ML interview, the discussion went far beyond the traditional RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) architecture. The panel explored how modern GenAI systems are evolving with KAG (Knowledge-Augmented Generation) and CAG (Cache-Augmented Generation).

🔹 RAG – Retrieves relevant information from external sources before generating a response.
🔹 KAG – Uses structured knowledge graphs and relationships, enabling deeper reasoning, better context understanding, and higher accuracy.
🔹 CAG – Reuses cached context and previously processed responses to reduce latency, improve efficiency, and lower operational costs.

💡 Key Takeaway:
RAG fetches what it needs.
KAG understands what it knows.
CAG reuses what it has.

As AI systems move toward production-scale deployments, understanding these architectures is becoming essential for AI/ML Engineers, Data Scientists, and GenAI Developers.
Every interview teaches something new. Today's lesson: GenAI is no longer just about prompts and RAG; it's about building intelligent, scalable, and efficient knowledge systems.



Day 8 Topic: LangChain — The Framework Behind Many AI Applications

Building an AI application involves much more than calling an LLM API.
That's where LangChain comes in.
LangChain is an open-source framework that helps developers build AI-powered applications by connecting LLMs with external tools, data sources, and workflows.
Some common use cases include:
🔹 Chatbots
🔹 RAG applications
🔹 Document Q&A systems
🔹 AI agents
🔹 Multi-step reasoning workflows
Key components of LangChain:
• Prompts
• LLMs
• Chains
• Retrievers
• Memory
• Tools & Agents
One thing I find interesting is that LangChain helps transform a standalone LLM into a complete AI application.
As AI systems become more complex, frameworks like LangChain are making it easier to build, orchestrate, and deploy intelligent workflows.


𝗬𝗼𝘂’𝗿𝗲 𝗼𝗽𝘁𝗶𝗺𝗶𝘇𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗺𝗼𝗱𝗲𝗹𝘀… 𝗯𝘂𝘁 𝗶𝗴𝗻𝗼𝗿𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗼𝗻𝗲 𝘁𝗵𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝘁𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝗮𝗰𝘁𝘂𝗮𝗹𝗹𝘆 𝗱𝗿𝗶𝘃𝗲𝘀 𝗰𝗼𝘀𝘁.

Adding more context feels like the right move…
But it silently increases latency, cost, and even reduces accuracy

That trade-off is what breaks real GenAI systems.

𝟭. 𝗧𝗼𝗸𝗲𝗻 𝗕𝘂𝗱𝗴𝗲𝘁𝗶𝗻𝗴
↳ Allocate tokens across system, query, docs, history
↳ Don’t send everything by default

𝟮. 𝗖𝗵𝘂𝗻𝗸𝗶𝗻𝗴 + 𝗥𝗲𝗿𝗮𝗻𝗸𝗶𝗻𝗴
↳ Split docs into smaller chunks
↳ Pass only the most relevant ones

𝟯. 𝗗𝘆𝗻𝗮𝗺𝗶𝗰 𝗥𝗲𝘁𝗿𝗶𝗲𝘃𝗮𝗹
↳ Fewer tokens for simple queries
↳ More context only when needed

𝟰. 𝗖𝗼𝗺𝗽𝗿𝗲𝘀𝘀𝗶𝗼𝗻
↳ Summarize chunks before sending
↳ Keep signal high, tokens low

𝟱. 𝗛𝗶𝘀𝘁𝗼𝗿𝘆 + 𝗖𝗮𝗰𝗵𝗶𝗻𝗴
↳ Compress chat history or use sliding window
↳ Cache repeated queries → zero token cost

𝟲. 𝗣𝗿𝗼𝗺𝗽𝘁 + 𝗢𝘂𝘁𝗽𝘂𝘁 𝗢𝗽𝘁𝗶𝗺𝗶𝘇𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻
↳ Short, precise prompts win
↳ Cap outputs to avoid token waste

Token optimization isn’t a trick… it’s how production AI actually works.
Which one are you missing in your system?

If you want to go deeper, this is just one layer. Once you connect these pieces, you start seeing the full flow of how real AI systems are built.

I built the same AI agent 6 times.

LangGraph vs CrewAI vs PydanticAI vs OpenAI SDK vs Smolagents vs Google ADK.

And only two made my final stack.

Every developer asks the same question: "Which agent framework should I learn?" The truth is they solve different problems.

Here's the honest comparison 👇

1. LangGraph — best for production workflows
State management, checkpointing, human approval loops, durable execution. Steeper learning curve. Great for enterprise pipelines and long-running jobs.

2. CrewAI — best for fast multi-agent prototypes
Spin up researcher / writer / reviewer teams. High token usage and messy debugging. Great for hackathons, demos and MVPs.

3. PydanticAI — best for backend engineers
Type safety, structured outputs, validation, FastAPI integration. No built-in orchestration. Great for APIs and extraction pipelines.

4. OpenAI Agents SDK — best for OpenAI ecosystems
Simple primitives: agents, tools, handoffs, guardrails. Smaller ecosystem. Great for tool calling and moderate complexity.

5. Smolagents — best for open source
Writes Python code instead of JSON tool calls. Lightweight and flexible — but needs sandboxing and security care.

6. Google ADK — best for GCP teams
Built-in evals, orchestration, Vertex AI integration. Gemini-first ecosystem.

My decision framework:
→ Single agent? PydanticAI
→ Complex workflow? LangGraph
→ Prototype by Friday? CrewAI
→ OpenAI stack? OpenAI SDK
→ Open source experiment? Smolagents
→ Google Cloud? Google ADK

The biggest lesson:
Framework choice matters less than prompt design, tool design, retrieval quality, observability and evaluation.

A great architecture beats a trendy framework every time.

My 2026 production stack: PydanticAI for single-agent systems, LangGraph for complex workflows. Everything else is situational.

What are you using for AI agents? Drop your stack below 👇
