Who's Exposed to AI Risk?

𝗕𝗲𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗲 𝗯𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗮𝘁 𝗔𝗜 𝗶𝗻 𝗷𝘂𝘀𝘁 𝟭 𝗺𝗶𝗻𝘂𝘁𝗲 𝗮 𝗱𝗮𝘆.
𝗚𝗲𝘁 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗔𝗜 𝗻𝗲𝘄𝘀𝗹𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝘀𝗺𝗮𝗿𝘁 𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗱𝗲𝗿𝘀 𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗱.
𝗦𝗶𝗴𝗻 𝘂𝗽 𝗳𝗿𝗲𝗲 𝗻𝗼𝘄 → aiforleaders.com

Original post:
__________

Which sectors are most exposed to AI risk? In our three-round Delphi study with 272 AI experts, we asked experts to assess which sectors are most vulnerable across 24 AI risk domains.

Rather than suggesting that each sector faces a unique set of AI risks, the findings suggest that many sectors are exposed to overlapping risks as AI becomes embedded in decision-critical systems.

Some sectors were considered especially exposed, including Information Technology, Finance and Insurance, National Security, Health Care and Social Assistance, and Educational Services.

💡 A few other key findings:

1️⃣ Under business as usual, experts assigned a ≥10% probability of catastrophic outcomes to 18 of 24 AI risk domains over the next 5 years.

2️⃣ Even assuming pragmatic mitigations, 5 risks remained above the 10% catastrophic threshold: dangerous AI capabilities, cyberattacks and weapons, environmental harm, inequality, and power centralization.

3️⃣ Information, finance, and national security were rated the sectors most vulnerable to AI risks.

🔗How can you engage? See our (fancy) new webpage for our interactive summaries of the findings and preprint, and please share with anyone working on AI risk, governance, or policy (links in comments).

This research is part of the MIT AI Risk Initiative, which aims to help society understand, prioritize, and manage risks from AI. The initiative includes the MIT AI Risk Repository, a living database of more than 1,700 AI risks, the AI Incident Tracker, a collaboration with the Responsible AI Collaborative, which connects risks to over 1,400 incidents, and the MIT AI Governance Map, which analyzes risk coverage across more than 1,000 laws, standards, policies, and other governance documents curated by the Center for Security and Emerging Technology (CSET).

This work was led by Alexander Saeri, Jess Graham, and Michael Noetel 🔸, with a lot of feedback and support from Neil Thompson and others at MIT FutureTech. Thanks to the 272 participants, who very generously contributed their expertise to make the findings possible.

Credit to Peter Slattery, PhD. Follow him for more


🚀 Understanding LangChain — The Bridge Between LLMs and Real-World AI Applications

I recently created a handwritten visual guide to simplify LangChain, one of the most powerful frameworks for building AI applications with Large Language Models (LLMs).

🔹 What is LangChain? LangChain helps developers connect LLMs with external data sources, memory, APIs, databases, and tools to build intelligent and context-aware applications.

🔹 Why is it Important? A standalone LLM can generate responses, but real-world AI applications require: 
✅ Memory
✅ Access to external knowledge
✅ Multi-step reasoning workflows
✅ Integration with APIs, databases, and search systems

🔹 Key Components • Chains
• Memory
• Agents
• Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)
• Tools & API Integrations

🔹 Common Applications 
📌 AI Chatbots with Memory
📌 Document Question Answering (PDFs, Notes, Research Papers)
📌 AI Agents and Assistants
📌 Data Analysis using Natural Language
📌 Workflow Automation
📌 Real-Time AI Systems

🔹 RAG Workflow Documents → Embeddings → Vector Database → Relevant Context Retrieval → LLM → Accurate Answer

Understanding LangChain is a crucial step for anyone working in Generative AI, NLP, LLM Engineering, AI Agents, and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG).


Most developers today are building "AI Wrappers." I build resilient, enterprise-grade AI systems. 🚀

Over the past week, I architected 𝗢𝗺𝗻𝗶𝗥𝗔𝗚: A completely serverless, multi-tenant Document Assistant. 

My goal wasn't just to make an AI that answers questions. My goal was to engineer a system that handles concurrency, aggressive browser security, and constrained cloud environments without breaking a sweat.

Here is the engineering breakdown of how I solved the hardest architectural hurdles:

🔐 𝗠𝗶𝗹𝗶𝘁𝗮𝗿𝘆-𝗚𝗿𝗮𝗱𝗲 𝗦𝗲𝗰𝘂𝗿𝗶𝘁𝘆 & 𝗜𝗱𝗲𝗻𝘁𝗶𝘁𝘆 𝗠𝗮𝗻𝗮𝗴𝗲𝗺𝗲𝗻𝘁:
Cloud environments like Hugging Face strictly block third-party cookies via iframes. This locks users out of their secure data. I bypassed this by engineering a cryptographic "Identity Teleporter"—syncing JWTs and Session Tokens directly with the UI state. 

The Result: Users get seamless ChatGPT-style access with distinct routing:
1. 𝗧𝗲𝗺𝗽𝗼𝗿𝗮𝗿𝘆 𝗖𝗵𝗮𝘁 (𝗘𝗽𝗵𝗲𝗺𝗲𝗿𝗮𝗹): Visiting the main URL opens a temporary session. If you attempt to close the tab, the UI intercepts with a strict warning pop-up: "Closing this will permanently erase your data," keeping databases pristine.
2. 𝗗𝗶𝗿𝗲𝗰𝘁 𝗨𝗥𝗟 (𝗣𝗲𝗿𝘀𝗶𝘀𝘁𝗲𝗻𝘁): Using the Full-Screen App link generates a secure Direct URL granting 3-day multi-tenant memory. You can safely close the tab, and your data seamlessly persists. 

⚡ 𝗛𝘆𝗽𝗲𝗿-𝗘𝗳𝗳𝗶𝗰𝗶𝗲𝗻𝘁 𝗣𝗿𝗼𝗰𝗲𝘀𝘀𝗶𝗻𝗴 (𝗙𝗼𝘂𝗻𝗱𝗲𝗿 𝗙𝗿𝗶𝗲𝗻𝗱𝗹𝘆):
To make this extremely cost-efficient, I built a Zero-Infrastructure pipeline. Instead of burning API credits on OpenAI embeddings, I implemented a local sentence-transformers model. I engineered a custom Parent-Child chunking algorithm to slice massive documents without token dilution, routing them to a stateless Qdrant Cloud Database.

🧠 𝗗𝘆𝗻𝗮𝗺𝗶𝗰 𝗠𝗲𝗺𝗼𝗿𝘆 & 𝗖𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗲𝘅𝘁:
I integrated sub-second Llama-3.1 hooks. The exact millisecond a user sends their first message, a background worker autonomously generates a dynamic chat title and stores continuous conversation memory.

🛠️ 𝗙𝘂𝗹𝗹 𝗗𝗮𝘁𝗮 𝗦𝗼𝘃𝗲𝗿𝗲𝗶𝗴𝗻𝘁𝘆:
Customers hate losing control of their data. I built a robust backend that allows users to instantly "Share Chat" via secure links, or hit a GDPR-compliant "Delete Chat & Data" button that aggressively scrubs their vectors from the Qdrant cluster.

I don't just prompt AI. I architect the infrastructure that makes AI scalable, secure, and production-ready.

Everyone is talking about Claude Code.

Almost nobody is using it properly.

Last week, I watched a founder spend ₹3.5L on developers to build an internal automation tool.

Claude Code built the first working version in 4 hours.

The lesson wasn't "AI replaced developers."

The lesson was this:
Most people use Claude Code like a chatbot.
Top engineers use it like a teammate.

Here's my practical Claude Code workflow:
1️⃣ Start with a PRODUCT requirement, not a coding task.
❌ Build a dashboard.
✅ Build a dashboard where sales managers can track leads, conversion rates, and revenue in real time.
The quality of the output depends on the clarity of the outcome.

2️⃣ Create a CLAUDE.md file.
This is the biggest unlock.
Include:
• Project overview
• Tech stack
• Coding standards
• Folder structure
• Business rules
• Do's and Don'ts
Claude becomes dramatically more consistent.

3️⃣ Let Claude explore before coding.
I always ask:
"Analyze the codebase first. Identify risks, dependencies, and implementation options before writing code."
This single step prevents hours of rework.

4️⃣ Use Claude for architecture, not just code generation.
My best results come from prompts like:
"Design the complete system architecture and implementation plan."
The code is easy.
The thinking is where the value is.

5️⃣ Make Claude review its own work.
Before accepting any output:
"Act as a senior engineer. Review this implementation. Find bugs, security issues, scalability concerns, and improvements."
The second pass is often better than the first.

6️⃣ Use it on the work you're avoiding.
Not the easy tasks.
The messy integrations.
The undocumented APIs.
The legacy code.
The migration projects.
That's where the biggest ROI lives.

Here's what most people still don't understand:
The future isn't developers vs AI.
It's developers with AI vs developers without AI.
And the gap is getting wider every month.
I'm seeing founders build MVPs in days.
Consultants automate weeks of work.
Small teams deliver enterprise-grade products.
The leverage is real.

Question for builders:
What's the most valuable thing you've built with Claude Code so far?

SEO vs AEO vs GEO

__________

ChatGPT just recommended your competitor to 10,000 people.

Your content didn't even get considered.

This is the new search reality
and most brands have no strategy for it.

In 2026, search lives in three places:

🟢 Google search results → SEO
🟣 AI-generated snippets & voice → AEO
🌿 ChatGPT, Gemini & AI citations → GEO

Here's the difference:

→ SEO builds your foundation. Keywords, backlinks, technical health. Still essential but no longer enough on its own.

→ AEO gets you into instant answers. Structure your content with answer-first copy, FAQ schema, and approx. 40–60 word responses. Be the snippet.

→ GEO gets you cited by AI. Clean structure, expert-level content (E-E-A-T), trusted sources, tables and FAQs that AI can summarise and reference.

The risk of ignoring any one of these?

— SEO alone: one algorithm update wipes your traffic
— AEO alone: users get answers without ever visiting you
— GEO alone: impossible to measure, hard to scale

2026 is about being credible, retrievable, and trusted everywhere.


🚀 LangChain vs LangGraph: Choosing the Right Framework for Agentic AI
As AI applications evolve beyond simple chatbots, the architecture behind them becomes increasingly important.

One comparison I found particularly useful is LangChain vs LangGraph.
🔹 LangChain is great for:
    - Sequential workflows
    - Simple RAG applications
    - Content generation
    - Traditional chatbot development
🔹 LangGraph shines when you need:
    - Complex, graph-based workflows
    - Persistent state management
    - Human-in-the-loop processes
    - Multi-agent systems
    - Dynamic decision-making and branching logic

The key takeaway?
LangGraph is not a replacement for LangChain—it's an evolution built on top of it.

Prompt injection is the #1 security risk for AI systems — and most teams aren't ready.

It doesn't attack your code. It attacks the model's instruction-following logic itself. No technical skill required. 👇

What is prompt injection?
Attackers embed hidden or deceptive instructions inside text an LLM processes — making it behave in unintended, often harmful ways.

Just like SQL injection slips malicious code into a query, prompt injection slips malicious instructions into an LLM's input — hijacking its behavior.

Why are LLMs vulnerable?
System prompt (trusted) + user input (untrusted) + external data (untrusted) are all processed as ONE block of text. No trust separation.
Core weakness: an LLM can't natively tell trusted instructions from untrusted content — injected commands run with the same authority as legitimate ones.

Two faces of the attack:
🎯 Direct — attacker talks to the model directly ("ignore all previous instructions"). Visible, fires immediately, easier to detect. Used for jailbreaks & DAN prompts.
📄 Indirect — attacker poisons external data (docs, emails, sites). Hidden from humans, fires when processed, much harder to detect. Used for poisoned RAG & résumés.
Newest threat: multimodal injection — malicious instructions hidden inside images, audio, or video.

It's already happening:
🔓 Bing Chat "Sydney" leak — told to ignore its instructions, it revealed its hidden system prompt.
🧓 The "Grandma" exploit — emotional role-play slipped right past safety filters.
📄 Résumé injection — invisible white-on-white "rate this candidate highly" fooled AI screening tools.
🧠 ChatGPT memory exploit — injected instructions persisted across sessions, enabling long-term data exfiltration.

Why leaders should care:
💸 Data leakage — system prompts, API keys, confidential context exposed
🛡️ Safety bypass — guardrails overridden, dangerous content produced
🔧 Tool & API abuse — if an LLM can touch email/files/DBs, injection can trigger unauthorized actions
⚖️ Privacy & compliance — leaked user data means GDPR / HIPAA liability
The agentic escalation: when an injected LLM can browse, run code, send email, or manage files, a single injection can trigger irreversible real-world actions.

How to defend — defense-in-depth:
1️⃣ Input sanitization & validation — treat all input as untrusted; strip hidden text & invisible characters
2️⃣ Prompt structure & delimiters — separate system instructions from user content; use API-level roles
3️⃣ Least-privilege access — grant only the tools the model truly needs; require human confirmation for sensitive actions
4️⃣ Output monitoring & filtering — never assume output is safe; scan responses, redact keys & PII
No single layer is enough — defense-in-depth is the only real answer.

Security isn't an afterthought. It's a feature.

One repost could help a colleague build safer AI — and dodge the #1 LLM security risk. ♻️

Follow me for more such insights on AI & tech in plain English.


🚀 How Do Large Language Models (LLMs) Work?

I created this infographic to simplify the journey of an LLM—from tokenization and embeddings to transformers, alignment (RLHF/DPO), and decoding strategies like Top-K, Top-P, and Temperature sampling.

Modern LLMs are trained on massive datasets, fine-tuned to follow instructions, aligned with human preferences, and generate responses one token at a time.

 🚀 Everyone wants to become an AI Engineer. Few understand what the job actually looks like.

Many people think AI Engineering is just:
✅ Using ChatGPT
✅ Writing prompts
✅ Getting instant answers

That's only the beginning.

Behind every production-ready AI application, an AI Engineer is responsible for:
🔹 Designing effective prompts
🔹 Building RAG pipelines
🔹 Integrating APIs, databases, and vector stores
🔹 Managing token usage and AI costs
🔹 Debugging failures and handling edge cases
🔹 Monitoring latency, reliability, and performance
🔹 Deploying secure, scalable AI systems

The difference is simple:

Using AI tools makes you an AI user.
Building reliable AI systems makes you an AI Engineer.

The future belongs to engineers who can take AI beyond demos and turn it into real-world products that people can trust.

💬 What do you think is the biggest misconception about becoming an AI Engineer?

📌 Interested in learning AI Engineering? Registration link is in the first comment.

Most AI developers still think RAG and Agentic RAG are the same thing.
They're not.
RAG retrieves information.
Agentic RAG plans, remembers, uses tools, and decides when to retrieve.
Multi-Agent RAG goes one step further by coordinating specialized agents to solve more complex tasks.
The future of enterprise AI isn't just better models.
It's better systems.
Which architecture are you building today?

About the job
We are looking for a highly skilled AI Engineer specializing in Large Language Models (LLMs) and Agentic AI. You will architect, build, and deploy production-grade LLM applications — from intelligent knowledge bases and RAG systems to autonomous multi-agent workflows. You will work hands-on with open-source Chinese and international LLMs (DeepSeek, Qwen, Kimi, etc), implementing everything from model deployment and inference optimization to prompt engineering and agent orchestration. This is a builder role for someone who thrives at the intersection of research and engineering.

Key Responsibilities

LLM Application Development

Design and develop enterprise LLM-powered applications: intelligent Q&A systems, enterprise knowledge base assistants, AI copilots, document analysis tools, and automated customer service agents.
Architect and implement end-to-end RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) systems: document parsing and chunking (recursive, semantic, agentic), embedding generation (BGE, M3E, GTE), vector retrieval (dense + sparse hybrid search), reranking (bge-reranker, Cohere Rerank), and response synthesis with source attribution.
Develop and optimize Prompt Engineering strategies: chain-of-thought, tree-of-thought, few-shot prompting, structured output parsing (JSON mode / Pydantic), prompt templates (LangChain/LangSmith), and prompt version management.
Knowledge in harness engineering, context management in ensuring LLM interactions and or AI agents reliable and deterministic.

AI Agent & Multi-Agent Systems

Design and build AI Agent systems using ReAct, Plan-and-Execute, Reflection, and multi-agent collaboration patterns.
Implement Function Calling and tool-use capabilities, enabling agents to interact with external APIs, databases, and enterprise systems.
Develop multi-agent orchestration using LangGraph, AutoGen, CrewAI, and other agent frameworks to solve complex enterprise tasks through agent collaboration.
Design MCP (Model Context Protocol) integrations for standardized LLM tool interoperability.

Open-Source LLM Deployment & Optimization

Deploy and optimize latest version of open-source Chinese LLMs: DeepSeek, Qwen, and Kimi for on-premise and private cloud environments.
Implement model inference optimization: quantization (GGUF/llama.cpp, GPTQ, AWQ, AutoAWQ, FP8/INT8), KV Cache optimization, continuous batching (vLLM, TensorRT-LLM, TGI, SGLang), speculative decoding, and tensor parallelism for high-throughput serving.
Build and maintain model serving infrastructure using vLLM, TensorRT-LLM, Text Generation Inference (TGI), Ollama, Xinference, and SGLang; configure GPU resource scheduling with Kubernetes + GPU operators. AI gateway tools for routing, model tracking and load balancing such as TrueFoundry, Kubeflow, LiteLLM or Ray for heavy deep learning.

Model Fine-Tuning & Customization

Implement efficient fine-tuning pipelines using LoRA, QLoRA, DoRA, and full-parameter fine-tuning on proprietary domain-specific datasets.
Prepare and curate instruction-following datasets, RLHF/RLAIF datasets, and evaluation benchmarks for domain adaptation.
Evaluate fine-tuned models using automated benchmarks and LLM-as-a-Judge methodologies.

Evaluation & Production Operations

Build and maintain LLM evaluation frameworks: LLM-as-a-Judge, RAGAS, DeepEval, ARES, and custom task-specific metrics for continuous quality monitoring.
Implement production monitoring for LLM systems: output quality tracking, latency/throughput metrics, cost monitoring, drift detection, and guardrail compliance.
Design A/B testing frameworks for model comparison and prompt iteration.
Implement LLM security guardrails: input/output filtering, PII detection, prompt injection defense, content moderation, and safety alignment.

Research & Technical Leadership

Track frontier AI research and evaluate emerging technologies (new model architectures, training techniques, inference methods) for enterprise adoption.
Contribute to internal knowledge sharing: tech talks, documentation, and best-practice guides on LLM development.

Required Qualifications

Bachelor's degree or above in Computer Science, Artificial Intelligence, Machine Learning, or related technical field. Master's or PhD in AI/ML preferred.
2+ years of professional experience in AI/ML engineering with demonstrated production deployment of LLM-based systems at scale.
Deep understanding of Transformer architecture, attention mechanisms (MHA, GQA, MQA), and LLM pre-training / fine-tuning / inference paradigms.
Expert proficiency in LLM application frameworks: LangChain, LlamaIndex, Haystack, or equivalent production-grade tools.
Hands-on experience with RAG system development: vector databases (Milvus, ChromaDB, Qdrant, Weaviate, Pinecone, pgvector), embedding models (BGE, M3E, GTE, OpenAI, Cohere), reranking (bge-reranker, Cohere Rerank, cross-encoders), and advanced retrieval techniques (hybrid search, query expansion, HyDE).
Practical experience deploying and tuning open-source Chinese LLMs: DeepSeek, Qwen, Kimi , or international models (Llama 3.x, Mistral, Mixtral, Gemma, Phi).
Strong experience with model deployment and serving infrastructure: vLLM, TensorRT-LLM, TGI, Ollama, Xinference, SGLang; GPU resource scheduling (Kubernetes + GPU operators).
Proficiency in model quantization and inference optimization: GGUF (llama.cpp), GPTQ, AWQ, AutoAWQ, FP8/INT8; knowledge of KV Cache optimization and memory-efficient attention (FlashAttention, FlashInfer, PageAttention).
Solid programming skills in Python; experience with PyTorch, TensorFlow, or JAX; familiarity with FastAPI/Flask for building LLM API services.
Experience with LLM evaluation methodologies, A/B testing frameworks, and production monitoring of AI systems.

Preferred Qualifications

Experience with agent frameworks: LangGraph, AutoGen, CrewAI, OpenAI Assistants API, and multi-agent orchestration patterns.
Familiarity with MCP (Model Context Protocol), OpenAI API specification, and multi-modal LLM capabilities (vision, audio).
Experience with prompt optimization tools: DSPy, PromptLayer, LangSmith for systematic prompt engineering.
Knowledge of model distillation and efficient transfer learning from large teacher models to smaller student models.
Contributions to open-source AI projects or publications in NLP/LLM research venues.
Experience with cloud GPU providers and cost optimization for LLM inference at scale.
Access to high-performance GPU computing resources for model development and experimentation.

SHIELD is a device-first fraud intelligence platform built on the world’s most persistent device identification technology across applications and platforms.

Powered by SHIELD AI and patented technology (SHIELD Sentinel), SHIELD sets the global standard for device identification (SHIELD Device ID) and exposes the full range of fraud tools and techniques through more than 20 risk indicators, delivering real-time, actionable fraud intelligence across all use cases and industries worldwide.

Trusted by global unicorns including inDrive, Alibaba, Deliveroo, Meesho, TrueMoney, Unico ID and OLX, SHIELD operates with teams across Los Angeles, São Paulo, London, Berlin, Jakarta, Bangkok, Bengaluru, Beijing and Singapore, advancing our mission: to eliminate unfairness and enable trust for the world.

For more information, visit shield.com.


Most RAG systems stop after retrieving a few relevant documents.

𝗔𝗴𝗲𝗻𝘁𝗶𝗰 𝗥𝗔𝗚 goes a step further. Instead of following a fixed retrieve and generate workflow, an AI agent can decide what to do next. It can refine the search, ask follow up questions, compare information from multiple sources, validate its findings, and even repeat the process until it has enough context to give a reliable answer.

Think of it as the difference between someone who grabs the first article they find and someone who researches a topic before responding.

This makes Agentic RAG especially useful for complex tasks where a single search is rarely enough.

The real shift is not just better retrieval. It is giving AI the ability to reason about how it retrieves information.

As AI applications become more capable, I believe this approach will become a key building block for building systems that are more accurate, reliable, and useful


The Rise of Local AI

𝗕𝗲𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗲 𝗯𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗮𝘁 𝗔𝗜 𝗶𝗻 𝗷𝘂𝘀𝘁 𝟭 𝗺𝗶𝗻𝘂𝘁𝗲 𝗮 𝗱𝗮𝘆.
𝗚𝗲𝘁 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗔𝗜 𝗻𝗲𝘄𝘀𝗹𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝘀𝗺𝗮𝗿𝘁 𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗱𝗲𝗿𝘀 𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗱.
𝗦𝗶𝗴𝗻 𝘂𝗽 𝗳𝗿𝗲𝗲 𝗻𝗼𝘄 → aiforleaders.com

Original post:
__________

The AI is coming back to your laptop.
Google's latest Gemma 4 12B launch is another strong signal of where AI infrastructure is heading:

Local AI.
For the last two years, most AI applications followed a simple pattern:

User → App → Cloud API → Response
But that pattern is changing.

Now we are moving toward:
User → Device → Local model → Cloud model when needed → Tools / Response

This shift is important because not every AI task needs a frontier cloud model.

Some tasks need speed.
Some need privacy.
Some need offline access.
Some need lower cost.
Some need to stay close to the user's data.

That is where local AI becomes powerful.
Cloud AI is still critical for large-scale reasoning, heavy workloads, centralized governance, and enterprise reliability.

Local AI is better for privacy-sensitive tasks, low-latency responses, personal productivity, offline workflows, and edge use cases.

Hybrid AI is where most real-world systems will eventually land.

The key architecture question will no longer be only:

"Which model API should we use?"
It will become:
"Where should this intelligence run?"

On the device?
In the browser?
In the private cloud?
In the public cloud?
Across all of them?

This is a big mindset shift for AI engineers.
Model selection is no longer enough.
Model placement is becoming a core architecture decision.

The winning systems will combine:

small local models
specialized edge models
enterprise RAG systems
cloud frontier models
governed tool access
human approval layers
observability across the full workflow

Local AI does not replace cloud AI.
It changes where intelligence runs.

And that means the laptop, phone, browser, and edge device are becoming part of the AI infrastructure stack.

The next wave of AI engineering will be about routing the right task to the right model in the right place.

The real question is:
What should run locally, and what should go to the cloud?

🚨 The biggest AI mistake people make today?
Using the same AI model for every task.

Most people ask:
❌ "Which AI model is the best?"

But the smarter question is:
✅ "Which AI model is best for this specific task?"

Because no single AI model wins at everything.
Each model shines in different situations:

🔍 Perplexity → Research & fact-finding
💻 Claude → Long documents, deep analysis & coding
📂 Gemini → Best for Google ecosystem workflows
🔒 Llama → Privacy, customization & control
⚡ ChatGPT → The best all-around daily AI assistant

The people getting the biggest advantage from AI are not using more tools.
They're using the right tool at the right time.

That's the difference between:
🧠 Using AI casually
vs
🚀 Using AI strategically

Using one AI for everything is like using:
🎨 One app for design
📊 Analytics
💬 Meetings
💻 Coding
✍️ Writing
🔍 Research
Can it work? Sure.
But is it the smartest approach? Probably not.

📌 AI becomes powerful when you stop treating it like a trend…
and start treating it like a skill stack.

The future belongs to people who know:
✔️ Which AI to use
✔️ When to use it
✔️ How to combine them effectively

That's when AI stops being another browser tab…
and becomes a real competitive advantage.

🚀 Understanding the Complete 𝐑𝐀𝐆 (𝐑𝐞𝐭𝐫𝐢𝐞𝐯𝐚𝐥-𝐀𝐮𝐠𝐦𝐞𝐧𝐭𝐞𝐝 𝐆𝐞𝐧𝐞𝐫𝐚𝐭𝐢𝐨𝐧) Pipeline
If you're building 𝐀𝐈 applications, 𝐋𝐋𝐌 chatbots, or 𝐄𝐧𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐩𝐫𝐢𝐬𝐞 AI systems, understanding the RAG pipeline is essential.
Here's how the process works:
📄 𝐃𝐨𝐜𝐮𝐦𝐞𝐧𝐭𝐬
 The pipeline starts with data sources such as PDFs, Word documents, text files, or any knowledge base.
✂️ 𝐂𝐡𝐮𝐧𝐤𝐢𝐧𝐠
 Large documents are divided into smaller, meaningful pieces called chunks. This improves retrieval accuracy and provides more relevant context.
🧠 𝐄𝐦𝐛𝐞𝐝𝐝𝐢𝐧𝐠𝐬
 Each chunk is converted into a numerical vector representation using an embedding model. These vectors capture the semantic meaning of the text instead of just matching keywords.
🗄️ 𝐕𝐞𝐜𝐭𝐨𝐫 𝐃𝐚𝐭𝐚𝐛𝐚𝐬𝐞
 The embeddings are stored in a Vector Database, enabling fast semantic search across thousands or even millions of document chunks.
❓ 𝐔𝐬𝐞𝐫 𝐐𝐮𝐞𝐫𝐲
 When a user asks a question, the query is transformed into an embedding using the same embedding model.
🔍 𝐒𝐢𝐦𝐢𝐥𝐚𝐫𝐢𝐭𝐲 𝐒𝐞𝐚𝐫𝐜𝐡
 The system compares the query embedding with the vectors stored in the database and retrieves the Top-K most relevant chunks.
🤖 𝐋𝐋𝐌 𝐆𝐞𝐧𝐞𝐫𝐚𝐭𝐢𝐨𝐧
 The retrieved context is combined with the user's question and sent to the Large Language Model (LLM), enabling it to generate an accurate, context-aware response.
💡 Why is RAG important?
 ✅ Reduces AI hallucinations
 ✅ Enables answers from your own data
 ✅ Keeps knowledge up to date without retraining the model
 ✅ Scales efficiently for enterprise applications
 ✅ Improves response accuracy and reliability
As AI Engineers, mastering 𝐑𝐀𝐆, 𝐄𝐦𝐛𝐞𝐝𝐝𝐢𝐧𝐠𝐬, 𝐕𝐞𝐜𝐭𝐨𝐫 𝐃𝐚𝐭𝐚𝐛𝐚𝐬𝐞𝐬, and 𝐋𝐋𝐌𝐬 is becoming a fundamental skill for building next-generation AI applications.


Agentic AI has a vocabulary problem.

𝗕𝗲𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗲 𝗯𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗮𝘁 𝗔𝗜 𝗶𝗻 𝗷𝘂𝘀𝘁 𝟭 𝗺𝗶𝗻𝘂𝘁𝗲 𝗮 𝗱𝗮𝘆.
𝗚𝗲𝘁 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗔𝗜 𝗻𝗲𝘄𝘀𝗹𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝘀𝗺𝗮𝗿𝘁 𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗱𝗲𝗿𝘀 𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗱.
𝗦𝗶𝗴𝗻 𝘂𝗽 𝗳𝗿𝗲𝗲 𝗻𝗼𝘄 → aiforleaders.com

This is the language your team needs first:

Before you build agents, define the words.

Ask 5 people on the same project what “agent” means.

You will probably get 5 different answers.

That becomes a problem when the agent can touch data, tools, customers, approvals, or live systems.

Use this as a quick audit before the next roadmap meeting.

Core building blocks:

→ MCP

Model Context Protocol connects agents to tools, systems, and data.

→ A2A

Agent-to-Agent protocol lets one agent coordinate work with another agent.

→ Agent Loop

The basic cycle: perceive, plan, act, observe, then repeat.

→ Tool Use

When an agent calls functions, APIs, apps, or internal systems.

→ Orchestrator

The layer that splits a goal across tools, agents, and humans.

System design terms:

→ Multi-Agent System

Several agents working together, with roles, routing, and shared goals.

→ Memory

Short-term context plus long-term knowledge the agent can reuse.

→ RAG

Retrieval-augmented generation, where the agent pulls external knowledge before answering.

→ Grounding

Tying outputs to trusted data so hallucinations become easier to catch.

→ Context Engineering

Designing exactly what the agent sees before it acts.

Control and risk terms:

→ Guardrails

Hard boundaries that stop risky actions, unsafe outputs, or unauthorised behaviour.

→ Policy Layer

Rules, permissions, and approvals that govern how agents operate.

→ Sandboxing

A safe test space before agents touch customers, payments, or live systems.

→ Human-in-the-Loop

Critical actions pause for a person to review, approve, or reject.

→ Handoffs

Clean transitions between agents, tools, teams, and human owners.

Production terms:

→ Agent Observability

Traces, logs, and monitoring that show what the agent actually did.

→ Agentic Pipeline

The structured path from input, to reasoning, to action, to output.

→ Evaluations

Tests that measure whether an agent is accurate, reliable, and ready.

→ Agent Identity

A record of which agent acted, under which role, with which permissions.

→ Agent Protocols

Shared standards that help agent systems work together at scale.

Bad vocabulary is only the first risk.

The bigger risk is teams building systems nobody can explain.

Start with shared language.
Then build the agent.
Then test the agent.

Which term would your team define differently today?

Open-source.

The fastest way to learn AI in 2026 is not by watching more tutorials.

It is by studying, using, and contributing to the projects shaping the ecosystem.

𝐇𝐞𝐫𝐞 𝐚𝐫𝐞 20 𝐀𝐈 𝐫𝐞𝐩𝐨𝐬𝐢𝐭𝐨𝐫𝐢𝐞𝐬 𝐰𝐨𝐫𝐭𝐡 𝐤𝐧𝐨𝐰𝐢𝐧𝐠:

→ Foundation Models

CrewAI, LangChain, Langflow, and AutoGPT help developers build intelligent agent workflows.

→ Local AI Infrastructure

→ RAG & Knowledge Systems
RAGFlow enables retrieval, grounding, and enterprise knowledge applications.

→ AI Workflow Automation
n8n and Dify simplify orchestration, automation, and AI application development.

→ AI Coding & Developer Tools
Claude Code, Gemini CLI, and OpenClaw are changing how developers build software.

→ Multimodal AI
Whisper, ComfyUI, and Stable Diffusion WebUI unlock speech, image, and generative media capabilities.

The biggest advantage in AI is no longer access to models.

It is understanding the ecosystem around them.

The engineers who move fastest are not learning one tool.

They are learning how the entire AI stack fits together.

In 2026, open-source AI is not just where innovation happens.

It is where future AI architects are built.

AI interviews have changed.
“Explain transformers.”
“Define RAG.”
“Difference between top-k and top-p.”
That is not enough anymore.

The real question is:
Can you think like an AI engineer?
Can you justify trade-offs?
Can you design systems?
Can you explain why you made a technical choice?

Because strong interviews are full of questions like:
→ When do you choose fine-tuning over prompting?
→ Why use RAG instead of training?
→ How do you prevent FastAPI bottlenecks with LLM workloads?
→ How would you architect GenAI systems for concurrent users?
→ How do memory, caching, retrieval, and latency fit together?

These are not memorization questions.
They test engineering judgment.

I put together an AI & GenAI Interview Kit covering the layers actually tested.
600+ questions across:
→ AI Fundamentals + Math
→ Transformers + LLM behavior
→ Prompting + Fine-Tuning
→ RAG + Memory Systems
→ System Design
→ Backend + Deployment
Built around practical reasoning, not textbook answers.


Most organisations are racing to adopt AI.

Very few are building the governance needed to scale it safely.

The challenge is no longer access to AI.

It is maintaining trust, accountability, security, and compliance as AI becomes embedded across the business.

Without governance, AI creates risk faster than it creates value.

The organisations leading in AI are treating governance as a strategic capability, not a compliance exercise.

𝐇𝐞𝐫𝐞 𝐚𝐫𝐞 8 𝐟𝐨𝐮𝐧𝐝𝐚𝐭𝐢𝐨𝐧𝐚𝐥 𝐜𝐚𝐩𝐚𝐛𝐢𝐥𝐢𝐭𝐢𝐞𝐬 𝐛𝐞𝐡𝐢𝐧𝐝 𝐞𝐟𝐟𝐞𝐜𝐭𝐢𝐯𝐞 𝐀𝐈 𝐠𝐨𝐯𝐞𝐫𝐧𝐚𝐧𝐜𝐞:

→ Data Catalog
Create visibility into critical data assets and ownership.

→ Data Lineage
Track how data moves, transforms, and influences AI outcomes.

→ Data Quality Checks
Ensure AI systems operate on accurate and reliable information.

→ Data Security
Protect sensitive data and reduce exposure risks.

→ Access Control
Limit access based on roles, responsibilities, and business needs.

→ Metadata Management
Standardise context and improve data discoverability.

→ Compliance Tracking
Monitor regulatory requirements and policy adherence.

→ Audit Logs
Maintain transparency, traceability, and accountability across AI systems.

As AI adoption accelerates, governance is becoming a competitive advantage.

Not because regulators demand it.

Because trustworthy AI is easier to scale, easier to secure, and easier to justify to stakeholders.

In 2026, the question is no longer:

"Do we need AI governance?"

The question is:

"Can we scale AI without it?"

The organisations that answer that question early will be the ones that capture the greatest value from AI.

I built a Claude agent team in 10 minutes.

𝗕𝗲𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗲 𝗯𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗮𝘁 𝗔𝗜 𝗶𝗻 𝗷𝘂𝘀𝘁 𝟭 𝗺𝗶𝗻𝘂𝘁𝗲 𝗮 𝗱𝗮𝘆.
𝗚𝗲𝘁 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗔𝗜 𝗻𝗲𝘄𝘀𝗹𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝘀𝗺𝗮𝗿𝘁 𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗱𝗲𝗿𝘀 𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗱.
𝗦𝗶𝗴𝗻 𝘂𝗽 𝗳𝗿𝗲𝗲 𝗻𝗼𝘄 → aiforleaders.com
__________

Not by writing better prompts.

By giving Claude the same structure a real team needs.

✅ Step 1: Install Claude Code

Pick the surface that matches your work.

→ Terminal if you already work in command lines
→ Desktop app if you want a normal Mac or Windows window
→ VS Code extension if your work sits inside an editor

Everything below this gets easier once the install is right.

✅ Step 2: Build context

Claude needs one onboarding doc before it can work properly.

→ Create ~/CLAUDE.md as the file Claude reads before each session
→ Add your role, voice, banned words, defaults, and output rules
→ Treat it like the briefing note you would give a new hire

Without context, every chat starts from zero again.

✅ Step 3: Build memory

Corrections should become system rules, not one-off feedback.

→ Save repeated corrections as separate .md files
→ Keep project memory inside .claude/projects/{project}/memory/
→ Index preferences, mistakes, and decisions in MEMORY.md

That is how the same mistake stops landing twice.

✅ Step 4: Build skills

A skill turns a repeated prompt into a single command.

→ Use /your-skill instead of pasting the same 200-word prompt
→ Connect the tools your work depends on, like Notion, Gmail, and Drive
→ Lock the output format so every run lands in the same shape

This is where Claude stops feeling like chat.

It starts feeling like a workflow you can reuse.

✅ Step 5: Build agents

One agent should not do every job.

→ Strategist uses Opus to analyse the task
→ Builder uses Sonnet to create the output
→ QA Gate fails anything below 95/100

The point is separation.

One part thinks.

One part builds.

One part checks the work before it reaches you.

✅ Step 6: Run on autopilot

Routines move the work off your laptop.

→ Daily 8am UK runs in Anthropic’s cloud
→ Weekly Monday drops the finished output into Notion
→ You set the schedule once, then stop babysitting the process

That is the difference between an AI user and an AI operator.

A user asks Claude for help.

An operator builds the system Claude can run.

Which step would save you the most time right now?

𝗔𝗿𝗴𝗼 𝗖𝗗 𝗔𝗿𝗰𝗵𝗶𝘁𝗲𝗰𝘁𝘂𝗿𝗲 𝗘𝘅𝗽𝗹𝗮𝗶𝗻𝗲𝗱

Argo CD brings GitOps to Kubernetes by treating Git as the single source of truth for application deployments.

Here's how the architecture works:

🔹 Developers commit application changes to Git.

🔹 The Repository Server retrieves the latest manifests from Git and renders them using Helm, Kustomize, plugins, or plain YAML.

🔹 The Application Controller continuously compares the desired state in Git with the live state in the Kubernetes cluster.

🔹 If drift is detected, Argo CD synchronizes the cluster automatically (if Auto Sync is enabled) or waits for a manual sync to bring the live state back in line with Git.

🔹 Sync Hooks can run custom tasks such as database migrations, smoke tests, notifications, or cleanup before, during, or after a deployment.

This continuous reconciliation process helps eliminate configuration drift, improve deployment reliability, and provide a fully auditable deployment workflow.

Git → Compare → Sync → Deploy → Verify

A simple concept that has become a core part of modern Kubernetes platform engineering.

Image credit: DevOpsCube


The real cost of API-hosted LLMs isn't on the pricing page.
Most teams pick API because it ships fast.
 
That's right early on.
But the decision gets expensive at scale.

Cost structure:
 → API: pay per token, zero upfront, expensive at high volume
 → Local: fixed GPU cost, near-zero marginal cost per request
 → Crossover: usually 3 to 5M tokens per month

Hidden costs nobody budgets for:
 → API: vendor lock-in, uncapped cost spikes, rate limit upgrades
 → Local: DevOps time, GPU idle hours, redeployment on upgrades

Latency reality:
 → API: fast average but 8 second spikes on shared infrastructure
 → Local: consistent 1.1 seconds, no noisy neighbors

Privacy:
 → API: data leaves your environment
 → Local: stays in-house, required for HIPAA, finance, legal

When to choose:
 → Choose API: fast to ship, low volume, no ops team
 → Choose Local: strict privacy, high consistent volume, fine-tuning needed

The move most production teams make:
Start with API.
Migrate high-volume stable endpoints to local once the pattern is clear.
Smart routing beats picking one side.

🎯MLOps vs LLMOps
How are these two AI pipelines different?

At first glance, they look similar.

Both involve deployment, monitoring, and continuous improvement.

But they solve very different problems.

MLOps

→ Starts with training data

→ Focuses on data preparation and feature engineering

→ Trains custom machine learning models

→ Tracks experiments and model versions

→ Monitors accuracy, drift, and retraining needs

→ Common tools: MLflow, Kubeflow, Airflow, SageMaker, Vertex AI

——

LLMOps

→ Starts with a foundation model

→ Focuses on prompts, context, and retrieval

→ Uses RAG and vector databases

→ Evaluates quality, safety, and hallucinations

→ Monitors cost, latency, and user feedback

→ Common tools: LangChain, LangGraph, LlamaIndex, vLLM, Weights & Biases, Pinecone.


𝐌𝐨𝐬𝐭 𝐝𝐚𝐭𝐚 𝐭𝐞𝐚𝐦𝐬 𝐝𝐨𝐧'𝐭 𝐟𝐚𝐢𝐥 𝐛𝐞𝐜𝐚𝐮𝐬𝐞 𝐭𝐡𝐞𝐲 𝐩𝐢𝐜𝐤𝐞𝐝 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐰𝐫𝐨𝐧𝐠 𝐭𝐞𝐜𝐡𝐧𝐨𝐥𝐨𝐠𝐲.

Because every architecture is optimized for a different business outcome.

Here's a practical guide to the 11 𝐦𝐨𝐬𝐭 𝐢𝐦𝐩𝐨𝐫𝐭𝐚𝐧𝐭 𝐝𝐚𝐭𝐚 𝐚𝐫𝐜𝐡𝐢𝐭𝐞𝐜𝐭𝐮𝐫𝐞 𝐩𝐚𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐧𝐬 every software engineer and tech leader should know:

→ Analytics → Operational systems

Most AI confusion starts with using one word for seven different layers.
 
𝗕𝗲𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗲 𝗯𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗮𝘁 𝗔𝗜 𝗶𝗻 𝗷𝘂𝘀𝘁 𝟭 𝗺𝗶𝗻𝘂𝘁𝗲 𝗮 𝗱𝗮𝘆.
𝗚𝗲𝘁 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗔𝗜 𝗻𝗲𝘄𝘀𝗹𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝘀𝗺𝗮𝗿𝘁 𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗱𝗲𝗿𝘀 𝗿𝗲𝗮𝗱.
𝗦𝗶𝗴𝗻 𝘂𝗽 𝗳𝗿𝗲𝗲 𝗻𝗼𝘄 → aiforleaders.com
__________
 
AI is not one thing.
It is a stack.
And leaders make better decisions when they know which layer they are actually talking about.
AI is the broad field.
It covers systems that can reason, learn, decide, or act intelligently.
Expert systems, robotics, game AI, and autonomous agents all sit inside that umbrella.
 
Machine learning is narrower.
ML learns patterns from data instead of following fixed rules.
That is why it shows up in spam detection, recommendation engines, fraud detection, customer segmentation, and predictive analytics.
Deep learning goes one level deeper.
DL uses multi-layer neural networks for harder pattern recognition problems.
Image recognition, speech recognition, self-driving systems, medical imaging, and translation all rely on that jump.
 
Generative AI is where most people entered the conversation.
It creates new text, images, audio, video, and code.
ChatGPT, Claude, Midjourney, DALL-E, Stable Diffusion, Suno, and Runway are examples people recognize because they produce visible outputs.
Large language models are a specific kind of GenAI.
GPT-4/5, Claude 3, Gemini 1.5, Llama 3, Mistral, and Qwen are built for understanding and generating language.
 
RAG is not another chatbot label.
Retrieval-Augmented Generation connects an LLM to external knowledge.
That is what makes enterprise chatbots, company copilots, document Q&A, and knowledge-base assistants more accurate and current.
Agentic AI adds action.
OpenAI Agents, Claude Computer Use, AutoGPT, LangGraph Agents, and Manus AI point toward systems that can plan, use tools, and complete multi-step work.
 
The simple sequence is:
AI
ML
DL
GenAI
LLMs
RAG
Agentic AI
 
That order matters because each layer changes the leadership question.
Are we trying to classify something?
Generate something?
Ground answers in company knowledge?
Or let a system take supervised action across tools?
Those are different architectures, risks, budgets, and success metrics.
 
Use GenAI when the job is content creation.
Use LLMs when language understanding is the core interface.
Use RAG when answers must reference trusted knowledge.
Use agents when the system must reason, plan, and execute.
 
The future is not just better prompts.
It is better matching between problem, model, data, retrieval, and action.

Which layer are you building with right now?

Taxonomy vs Ontology vs Semantic Mapping vs Knowledge Graph.
I see these terms getting mixed up a lot in enterprise AI and data architecture discussions.
They sound similar, but they are solving different problems.
The way I look at it:

Taxonomy = organize the information
It gives a clean structure.
Example:
Product > Electronics > Laptop > Ultrabook
It helps with classification, navigation, reporting, and standardization.

Ontology = define the meaning
It explains the concepts, properties, and relationships.
Example:
Customer places an order
Order contains Product
Product belongs to Category
This is where the business meaning starts to become clear.

Semantic Mapping = align different systems
Most enterprises have the same concept stored in different ways across systems.
Example:
CRM says Customer
ERP says Client
Support system says Account
Semantic mapping connects these terms to a common business meaning.

Knowledge Graph = activate the connected knowledge
This is where entities and relationships come together as a network.
Example:
Customer → placed → Order
Order → contains → Product
Product → belongs to → Category
Customer → raised → Support Ticket
Now AI systems can use the relationships, not just isolated records.
That is the important part.
A knowledge graph is not just a graph database.
Without proper taxonomy, ontology, and semantic mapping, it can easily become another messy data layer.
For enterprise AI, especially RAG, agents, semantic search, recommendation, and decision intelligence, this foundation matters a lot.

To sum it up in one line each:

Taxonomy organizes the structure.
Ontology defines the meaning.
Semantic mapping aligns across systems.
Knowledge graph connects everything into usable context.


𝐓𝐡𝐞 𝐬𝐦𝐚𝐫𝐭𝐞𝐬𝐭 𝐀𝐈 𝐬𝐲𝐬𝐭𝐞𝐦𝐬 𝐝𝐨𝐧'𝐭 𝐬𝐮𝐜𝐜𝐞𝐞𝐝 𝐛𝐞𝐜𝐚𝐮𝐬𝐞 𝐨𝐟 𝐛𝐞𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐫 𝐩𝐫𝐨𝐦𝐩𝐭𝐬.

𝐓𝐡𝐞𝐲 𝐬𝐮𝐜𝐜𝐞𝐞𝐝 𝐛𝐞𝐜𝐚𝐮𝐬𝐞 𝐞𝐯𝐞𝐫𝐲 𝐜𝐨𝐦𝐩𝐨𝐧𝐞𝐧𝐭 𝐤𝐧𝐨𝐰𝐬 𝐡𝐨𝐰 𝐭𝐨 𝐜𝐨𝐦𝐦𝐮𝐧𝐢𝐜𝐚𝐭𝐞.

Most people focus on the LLM.

Production AI systems are much bigger than that.

Every request moves through multiple layers before a response reaches the user:
• User intent is understood.
• The right protocol is selected.
• Relevant tools and data are accessed.
• Context and memory are retrieved.
• The model reasons over the available information.
• A grounded response is generated and delivered.

Different communication protocols solve different problems:
• REST APIs → Standard request-response communication.
• MCP (Model Context Protocol) → Connects AI models with tools and external data.
• WebSockets → Real-time, bidirectional communication.
• SQL → Structured access to relational data.
• Event Streaming → Asynchronous communication for scalable systems.

A production-ready AI architecture typically includes:
• Agent orchestration
• Planning and reasoning
• Memory and context management
• Retrieval and data access
• Tool execution
• Model inference and response generation

The takeaway is simple:

AI isn't just about choosing the best model.

It's about designing reliable communication between models, memory, tools, APIs, databases, and users.

The better your communication architecture, the more reliable your AI system becomes.

Which protocol do you rely on the most today REST APIs, MCP, WebSockets, or Event Streaming?

Military Cybersecurity is the practice of defending military computer networks, systems, and data from cyber threats through prevention, detection, and response. It involves protecting critical infrastructure, maintaining operational readiness, and conducting offensive and defensive cyber warfare operations. Specialized roles like cybersecurity specialists and integrated agencies are established to handle these responsibilities. 

📌 Industry Dynamics & Segment Leadership
Network security continues to dominate with a 45% market share, followed by endpoint security at 30% and cloud security at 25%. These numbers highlight the global defense sector’s heightened focus on safeguarding mission-critical infrastructures from increasingly sophisticated cyber threats.

📌 Key Industry Developments

Lockheed Martin formed a new strategic cybersecurity partnership to integrate advanced threat detection into its systems.

Raytheon Technologies launched an AI-powered cybersecurity suite specifically engineered for military-grade protection.

BAE Systems secured major contracts in Asia-Pacific, reinforcing its influence in emerging defense markets.

The U.S. Department of Defense continues to strengthen regulations, pushing for stricter compliance and more resilient cyber architectures.

📌 Trends Shaping the Future
Artificial intelligence, predictive analytics, and machine learning are driving next-generation threat intelligence. Blockchain is emerging as a secure framework for ata exchange, while cloud-based defense cybersecurity is gaining significant adoption. Nations worldwide are prioritizing cyber resilience to ensure uninterrupted operations and national security. As global cyber warfare intensifies, military organizations are investing heavily in advanced defenses—reshaping the competitive landscape and accelerating technological innovation

𝗣𝗛𝗔𝗡𝗧𝗢𝗠 offers 47'524 adversarial image-text pairs spanning 7'826 harmful intents across 10 categories and 55 subcategories, generated with five attack strategies (BAP, IDEATOR, MML, FC_ATTACK, CSDJ) in single- and multi-turn settings against six open-source VLMs (DeepSeek-VL2, GLM-4.6V-Flash, Kimi-VL-A3B-Instruct, Qwen3-VL-30B-A3B-Instruct, Qwen3.5-27B, Qwen3.6-27B) and transferred to six proprietary models (GPT-5.4 and 5.5, Claude Opus 4.6, 4.7 and 4.8, Gemini 3.1 Pro).

Developed at the The Italian Institute of Artificial Intelligence (AI4I) in collaboration with HikmaAI.

Thanks to the team: Simone Gallivanone, Hossein Khodadadi, Mauro Dore and Mauro Medda

Dataset: https://lnkd.in/dH_5N_FD
Paper: https://lnkd.in/dnzVghAc


Continual learning — one AI direction I am most excited about.

We’re starting to see serious research momentum from Google Research and other labs.

The core idea is simple:
------ If AI systems are meant to operate continuously, learning itself can’t remain frozen.

Today, most model intelligence still largely relies on pretraining and fine-tuning.
Once a model is shipped, updating it is hard.

(You can think of the core model’s weights as being effectively frozen at release.

Even when we connect models to fresh information through tools like RAG, the model itself isn’t actually learning, its underlying parameters stay the same.)

Try to retrain parts of a deployed model and you often run into catastrophic forgetting:
------ new learning overwrites old knowledge, and the model can get worse, not better.

That’s why most models remain static, and why “learning” is treated as a one-off event rather than a continuous process.


𝗖𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗶𝗻𝘂𝗮𝗹 𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗿𝗻𝗶𝗻𝗴 asks a different question:

Can AI systems keep learning after deployment - incrementally, safely, and without resetting or retraining from scratch?

It’s a natural question to ask.
Humans don’t learn all at once, we adapt continuously over time.

Google Research explores nested learning - separating fast adaptation from slower, more stable knowledge to reduce forgetting, especially in long-context settings.

They has also explored memory architectures such as Titans and MIRAS, where a dedicated memory component can update while the system is running.

Leaders at Google DeepMind and Anthropic have both publicly noted that continual learning remains a missing capability, but not one with obvious fundamental blockers.


I like to think of continual learning as one of the missing foundations for truly agentic AI; without it, agents can act and remember, but not genuinely improve over time.

Curious how others see this - especially if you’re building agents or long-running AI systems?

https://lnkd.in/gMDUVAtk

Something is quietly redefining how engineering systems operate, and most teams are still using only a fraction of it.

The shift is no longer about writing code faster.

It is about how execution itself is structured, controlled, and scaled.

𝐇𝐞𝐫𝐞 𝐢𝐬 𝐰𝐡𝐚𝐭 𝐦𝐨𝐝𝐞𝐫𝐧 𝐂𝐥𝐚𝐮𝐝𝐞 𝐬𝐭𝐲𝐥𝐞 𝐞𝐧𝐠𝐢𝐧𝐞𝐞𝐫𝐢𝐧𝐠 𝐰𝐨𝐫𝐤𝐟𝐥𝐨𝐰𝐬 𝐚𝐫𝐞 𝐚𝐜𝐭𝐮𝐚𝐥𝐥𝐲 𝐛𝐮𝐢𝐥𝐭 𝐨𝐧.

→ 𝐄𝐗𝐄𝐂𝐔𝐓𝐈𝐎𝐍 𝐏𝐋𝐀𝐍𝐍𝐈𝐍𝐆 𝐋𝐀𝐘𝐄𝐑
• Creates step by step execution flow before anything runs
• Enables structured review and validation upfront
• Reduces misalignment between intent and output

→ 𝐒𝐔𝐁𝐀𝐆𝐄𝐍𝐓𝐒
• Breaks complex work into multiple focused agents
• Runs tasks in parallel for efficiency
• Improves handling of large workflow systems

→ 𝐂𝐎𝐌𝐏𝐀𝐂𝐓𝐈𝐎𝐍
• Compresses conversation memory intelligently
• Keeps only high value context
• Improves long running system stability

→ 𝐒𝐋𝐀𝐒𝐇 𝐂𝐎𝐌𝐌𝐀𝐍𝐃𝐒
• Enables quick execution through predefined commands
• Reduces repetitive operational effort
• Standardizes frequent actions

→ 𝐂𝐎𝐍𝐓𝐄𝐗𝐓 𝐌𝐀𝐍𝐀𝐆𝐄𝐌𝐄𝐍𝐓
• Tracks files, history, and inputs continuously
• Ensures execution is always context aware
• Improves decision accuracy across tasks

→ 𝐏𝐋𝐔𝐆𝐈𝐍𝐒
• Extends system capabilities without core changes
• Adds modular functionality
• Supports scalable integration patterns

→ 𝐌𝐂𝐏 𝐋𝐀𝐘𝐄𝐑
• Standard protocol for tool connectivity
• Enables seamless external system integration
• Improves interoperability across services

→ 𝐇𝐎𝐎𝐊𝐒
• Automates actions based on events
• Enables dynamic workflow customization
• Adds reactive intelligence to pipelines

→ 𝐒𝐊𝐈𝐋𝐋𝐒
• Reusable execution templates
• Standardizes repetitive workflows
• Improves delivery speed and consistency

→ 𝐂𝐇𝐄𝐂𝐊𝐏𝐎𝐈𝐍𝐓𝐒
• Saves progress at key execution stages
• Enables rollback when needed
• Adds reliability to long workflows

→ 𝐏𝐋𝐀𝐍 𝐌𝐎𝐃𝐄 𝐀𝐍𝐃 𝐏𝐄𝐑𝐌𝐈𝐒𝐒𝐈𝐎𝐍𝐒
• Defines controlled execution boundaries
• Manages tool access safely
• Prevents unintended operations

→ 𝐂𝐋𝐀𝐔𝐃𝐄 𝐌𝐃 𝐂𝐎𝐍𝐅𝐈𝐆𝐔𝐑𝐀𝐓𝐈𝐎𝐍
• Stores project level instructions
• Ensures consistent AI behavior
• Aligns outputs with system rules

The pattern is clear.

Modern AI engineering is not about isolated tools.

It is about orchestrated systems with memory, control, and modular execution.

PS: The real advantage is not access to tools. It is understanding how these layers interact to produce reliable outcomes at scale.

Most people talk about AI… 
but very few understand the system behind it.
Here’s the simplest breakdown of how modern AI actually works 👇

1️⃣ LLMs: the thinking layer

Models like GPT, Claude, Gemini, Mistral.
They understand language and generate ideas.
But they still need structure around them.

2️⃣ Frameworks: the wiring

LangChain, LlamaIndex, Haystack.
They connect your model to tools, data, memory, and workflows.
This is what turns a model into a system.

3️⃣ Vector Databases: the memory

Pinecone, Weaviate, Chroma.
They store meaning, not just text.
This is how AI retrieves information with context.

4️⃣ Data Extraction: the input pipeline

FireCrawl, Crawl4AI, Docling.
They pull information from messy sources
and turn it into clean data your model can use.

5️⃣ Open LLM Runtimes: the control layer

Hugging Face, Ollama, Groq.
Run models locally, privately, or at high speed.
More flexibility. Less lock-in.

6️⃣ Embeddings: the meaning engine

OpenAI, SBERT, Voyage, Cohere.
They convert text into vectors
so AI can compare ideas and understand relationships.

7️⃣ Evaluation: the quality check

Giskard, Ragas, TruLens.
You don’t know if your system works
until you measure accuracy, reasoning, and reliability.

AI isn’t one tool.
It’s an ecosystem.
And the people who understand the ecosystem
will be the ones who shape what their teams build next.

A simple playbook for leaders

Step 1 → Learn what each layer does
You don’t need to code.
Just understand the function.

Step 2 → Start with a workflow, not a tool
Pick one bottleneck.
Map the layers that solve it.

Step 3 → Build small, working systems first
One model + one framework + one memory layer
is enough to start.

Step 4 → Evaluate early
Don’t trust output you can’t measure.

Step 5 → Scale what works
Once the pipeline is stable, automate and expand.

Understanding the stack gives you leverage,
not overwhelm.

Which layer do you want to explore next?


𝐌𝐨𝐬𝐭 𝐜𝐨𝐦𝐩𝐚𝐧𝐢𝐞𝐬 𝐡𝐚𝐯𝐞 𝐧𝐨 𝐢𝐝𝐞𝐚 𝐰𝐡𝐲 𝐭𝐡𝐞𝐢𝐫 𝐀𝐈 𝐜𝐨𝐬𝐭𝐬 𝐤𝐞𝐞𝐩 𝐫𝐢𝐬𝐢𝐧𝐠.

The real cost explosion happens inside the architecture.

𝐈𝐧 𝐭𝐡𝐢𝐬, 𝐈 𝐛𝐫𝐞𝐚𝐤 𝐝𝐨𝐰𝐧 7 𝐡𝐢𝐝𝐝𝐞𝐧 𝐜𝐨𝐬𝐭 𝐝𝐫𝐢𝐯𝐞𝐫𝐬 𝐭𝐡𝐚𝐭 𝐪𝐮𝐢𝐞𝐭𝐥𝐲 𝐝𝐫𝐚𝐢𝐧 𝐀𝐈 𝐛𝐮𝐝𝐠𝐞𝐭𝐬:

• Context Window Bloat
→ Excessive context increases token spend.

• Agent Orchestration Overhead
 → Multi-agent workflows multiply model calls.

• Poor Model Routing
 → Premium models handle low-value tasks.

• Retrieval Inefficiencies
 → Poor RAG design increases compute usage.

• LLMOps & Observability
→ Monitoring and evaluations add operational overhead.

• Infrastructure & Runtime 
→ GPUs, storage, and scaling drive ongoing costs.

• Governance & Compliance
→ Security, audits, and regulatory controls require investment.

The biggest AI cost problems are not technical.
They are architectural.

The organisations reducing AI spend in 2026 are not using fewer models.

They are building more efficient systems.

If you are only tracking tokens, you are probably missing the real cost drivers


→ 𝐓𝐡𝐞 𝐇𝐢𝐝𝐝𝐞𝐧 𝐖𝐨𝐫𝐥𝐝 𝐈𝐧𝐬𝐢𝐝𝐞 𝐚𝐧 𝐀𝐈-𝐀𝐠𝐞𝐧𝐭

Ever wondered what truly powers an AI-agent beyond the flashy demos and headlines? 

The answer lies in its invisible anatomy - a carefully orchestrated system that thinks, acts, and evolves.

• Sensors (Perception) - This is how AI sees the world. From cameras to APIs, sensors gather the raw data that fuels understanding. Without perception, decisions are blind.

• Internal State / Memory - Every agent has a “mind.” It stores knowledge, context, and experiences. This memory guides decisions, allowing continuity beyond single interactions.

• Decision-Making / Reasoning - This is the brain of the agent. Algorithms analyze data, weigh options, and select actions aligned with its goals. It’s where intelligence emerges.

• Actuators (Action Mechanism) - Thoughts are meaningless without action. Actuators convert decisions into tangible outcomes - sending commands, triggering processes, or interacting with humans.

• Goal / Objective Module - Every AI needs a purpose. Goals define its behavior, ensuring every perception and action is directed toward measurable outcomes.

• Learning Component (Optional) - Some agents evolve over time. Machine learning allows adaptation, improving decisions with experience and new data.

• Communication Module (Optional) - Collaboration is key. Agents may exchange information with humans or other agents to achieve complex objectives.

• Evaluation / Monitoring Module (Optional) - Feedback loops matter. Continuous monitoring ensures alignment with objectives and helps correct mistakes before they escalate.

Understanding these components is not just technical - it’s strategic. 

Whether you are building AI solutions or integrating them into business, knowing the anatomy of an AI-agent is the first step toward mastery.

What is Forward Deployed Engineering (FDE)?

Everyone is talking about AI models. But the real challenge isn’t building models—it’s deploying AI that solves real customer problems.

That’s where Forward Deployed Engineers (FDEs) come in.

An FDE combines the skills of a software engineer, solution architect, AI engineer, and trusted customer advisor. They work directly with customers to understand business challenges, rapidly build AI solutions, and take them from prototype to production.

As enterprises accelerate AI adoption, I believe FDE will become one of the fastest-growing roles in tech over the next few years.

If you’re already a Software Engineer, Data Scientist, ML Engineer, Solutions Architect, or Cloud Engineer, now is a great time to start learning the skills needed for this role.

The Top Problem With AI Eval Platforms Nobody Wants to Say Out Loud
Parag Agarwal, PhD
Parag Agarwal, PhD
Software Engineer | Distributed Systems | AI Systems


July 1, 2026
Your eval dashboard is green. Faithfulness is 0.91, answer relevance is 0.88, the regression suite passed, and you shipped anyway. Two days later a customer forwards a screenshot of your agent confidently inventing a refund policy that has never existed. The dashboard is still green.

If you've run an LLM product in production, you've lived some version of this. And it points at the one problem that sits underneath every other complaint about eval platforms — the sampling gaps, the flaky judges, the dashboards nobody reads. That problem is simple to state and deeply uncomfortable to sit with:

You do not have ground truth. And most eval platforms are built to help you forget that.

Everything else is a symptom.

Evals are a measurement problem pretending to be a tooling problem
Classical ML had it easy, relatively speaking. You had labels. The image was a cat or it wasn't. The transaction was fraud or it wasn't. You could compute accuracy, precision, recall against a fixed answer key, and the number meant something because the answer key was real.

Generative systems broke the answer key. There is no single correct summary of a document. There is no canonical "right" response to a support ticket, a code review comment, or an agent's fourth tool call. Quality is multi-dimensional, context-dependent, and often only recognizable after the fact by a human who knows the domain.

So the honest situation is: the thing you most want to measure — "is this output actually good for this user in this context" — has no cheap, reliable, automatic label. That's not a gap in your tooling. That's the nature of the problem.

Eval platforms don't solve this. They can't. What they do is give you an interface, a database, and a set of proxies that feel like measurement. That's genuinely useful — but only if you never lose track of the fact that you're measuring proxies. Most teams lose track by week three.

LLM-as-judge is a loan, not income
The industry's answer to "we have no ground truth" was: use a bigger model to grade the smaller one. LLM-as-judge is now the default scoring mechanism baked into nearly every eval platform, and it is the single most misunderstood tool in the stack.

Here's the problem stated plainly. You are using an unreliable, non-deterministic system to measure the quality of an unreliable, non-deterministic system. When your judge says an output scores 0.9 on "faithfulness," you have not measured faithfulness. You have measured what one particular model, under one particular prompt, on one particular day, guessed about faithfulness. Change the judge model, change the rubric wording, change the temperature, and your "objective" score moves.

This isn't a reason to never use LLM judges. It's a reason to treat the score like a loan rather than income. It borrows credibility from the judge model, and that debt comes due the moment:

The judge shares failure modes with the model under test (they're often from the same family — same blind spots, same sycophancy, same formatting biases).
The judge rewards plausible-sounding over correct, because that's exactly what these models are trained to prefer.
Someone on your team quietly tunes the judge prompt until the numbers look good, which is Goodhart's law wearing a lab coat.

The platforms will happily let you stand up a dozen LLM-judged metrics in an afternoon. Almost none of them force you to answer the one question that matters: has anyone confirmed that this judge actually agrees with a human who knows the domain? Until you've measured judge-human agreement on a real labeled slice, your "eval" is a vibe with a decimal point.

Your eval set is not your production distribution
The second half of the ground-truth problem is coverage. Even if your scoring were perfect, you are only scoring the inputs you thought to include.

Eval sets are built the way most test suites are built: from the cases someone remembered, the bugs that already burned us, and a handful of adversarial prompts written in a good mood on a Tuesday. Production traffic is a living, drifting distribution shaped by real users doing things you did not anticipate — new slang, new jailbreak patterns, a partner integration sending malformed context, a seasonal spike in a query type you undersampled.

The result is a systematic and invisible mismatch. Your offline suite over-represents the failures you already understand and under-represents the ones about to hurt you. A green dashboard against a stale eval set is one of the most confidently wrong signals in all of software. It doesn't say "the system is good." It says "the system still passes the cases we thought of a month ago."

This is why the eval-then-ship regression story keeps repeating. The eval wasn't wrong. It was answering a different question than the one you thought you asked.

Where the platforms are genuinely good (credit where it's due)
None of this means eval platforms are a scam. The good ones — the Langfuses, Braintrusts, Arizes of the world — earn their keep on the parts of the problem that are tractable:

Tracing and observability. Capturing full production traces, inputs, outputs, tool calls, and latency is real, valuable engineering, and it's the foundation everything else should sit on.
Datasets and versioning. Turning interesting production traces into curated test cases, and versioning them, is exactly the right muscle to build.
Experiment plumbing. Running variant A against variant B across a fixed set and diffing the results is legitimately faster than the notebook-and-spreadsheet workflow it replaces.
Human-in-the-loop workflows. The better platforms make it easy to route outputs to human reviewers and capture those labels. This is the part most teams skip and shouldn't.

Notice the pattern: platforms are strongest where they help you collect, organize, and route evidence, and weakest exactly where they promise to automatically judge quality for you. The tooling is good. The autonomous verdict is the mirage.

What to actually do about it
Not a listicle of hot metrics. Four habits that respect the ground-truth problem instead of hiding it.

1. Anchor every automated metric to a human-labeled slice. Before you trust an LLM judge, label a few hundred real examples by hand — ideally with someone who understands the domain — and measure how often the judge agrees. If judge-human agreement is weak, your metric is decorative. This step is annoying, which is exactly why almost nobody does it, which is exactly why it's your edge.

2. Feed your eval set from production, continuously. Treat your dataset as a pipeline, not an artifact. Mine real traces, especially the weird and the failed ones, and promote them into the suite on a schedule. An eval set that doesn't drift with your traffic is decaying by definition.

3. Separate "did it break" from "is it good." Cheap, deterministic checks — schema validation, does-it-contain-the-required-disclaimer, did-the-tool-call-parse, is-it-under-the-token-limit — are underrated and reliable because they don't need a judge. Lean on them hard for the objective stuff, and reserve expensive, fuzzy LLM-judged evals for the genuinely subjective quality questions where nothing else works.

4. Keep a human in the loop at the point of highest stakes. Full manual review doesn't scale, and it doesn't have to. Sample. Spot-check the high-risk paths. The goal isn't to review everything; it's to make sure the automated numbers never fully detach from what a real person, looking at real output, would call good.

The bottom line
The top problem with AI eval platforms isn't a missing feature or the wrong metric or a judge that needs a better prompt. It's that the hardest part of evaluation — knowing what "correct" even means for open-ended generation — is a problem the platforms cannot solve for you, and their polished dashboards make it dangerously easy to believe they already have.

A green dashboard is not evidence of quality. It's evidence that your proxies are satisfied. Whether those proxies track reality is a question you have to answer yourself, with human judgment, on a distribution that keeps moving.

Buy the tracing. Buy the datasets. Buy the experiment plumbing. Just don't buy the idea that any platform has your ground truth. It doesn't. Nobody's does. And the teams that ship the most reliable AI are the ones who never let themselves forget it.

Written for practitioners who've watched a green dashboard lie to them. If this resonated, the argument continues over at BuildGeek AI.


→ 𝐌𝐮𝐥𝐭𝐢-𝐀𝐠𝐞𝐧𝐭 𝐒𝐲𝐬𝐭𝐞𝐦𝐬 𝐀𝐫𝐞 𝐍𝐨𝐭 𝐎𝐧𝐞 𝐀𝐫𝐜𝐡𝐢𝐭𝐞𝐜𝐭𝐮𝐫𝐞. 𝐓𝐡𝐞𝐲 𝐀𝐫𝐞 𝐚 𝐃𝐞𝐬𝐢𝐠𝐧 𝐂𝐡𝐨𝐢𝐜𝐞.

Most teams treat orchestration as implementation detail.
In reality, it defines system intelligence, failure behavior, and scale ceiling.

𝐇𝐞𝐫𝐞’𝐬 𝐡𝐨𝐰 𝐜𝐨𝐨𝐫𝐝𝐢𝐧𝐚𝐭𝐢𝐨𝐧 𝐩𝐚𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐧𝐬 𝐚𝐜𝐭𝐮𝐚𝐥𝐥𝐲 𝐝𝐢𝐟𝐟𝐞𝐫 𝐢𝐧 𝐩𝐫𝐨𝐝𝐮𝐜𝐭𝐢𝐨𝐧 𝐀𝐈 𝐬𝐲𝐬𝐭𝐞𝐦𝐬:

• Hierarchical Pattern
✓ Top-down control with planner and executors
✓ Clear task decomposition and supervision
✓ Predictable but can bottleneck at the orchestrator
✓ Single point of coordination risk

• Peer-to-Peer Mesh
✓ Agents interact directly without central control
✓ Fast context sharing across nodes
✓ Highly flexible but harder to govern
✓ Fault containment depends on local design quality

• Swarm Pattern
✓ No explicit coordination layer
✓ Simple agents follow local rules
✓ Emergent global behavior from interactions
✓ Strong resilience, weak predictability

• Supervisor Pattern
✓ Central orchestrator assigns, monitors, and aggregates
✓ Strong visibility and control over workflows
✓ Efficient for structured pipelines
✓ Limited adaptability under dynamic conditions

→ 𝐊𝐞𝐲 𝐢𝐧𝐬𝐢𝐠𝐡𝐭
The trade-off is not “better or worse architecture.”
It is control vs autonomy, and predictability vs emergence.

Most failures in multi-agent systems are not model failures.
They are coordination design failures.

P.S. How are you deciding orchestration patterns in your AI systems today-centralized control, distributed autonomy, or hybrid design?

I spent the last while breaking down what it actually takes to go from “I can call the OpenAI API” to “I can ship a production AI system.”

Turns out the gap isn’t prompting. It’s everything around the model:
→ Async Python + FastAPI so your backend doesn’t choke under load
→ Pydantic + structured outputs so the LLM stops returning garbage JSON
→ RAG pipelines (chunking, embeddings, vector DBs) so it stops hallucinating
→ Agents + the ReAct loop so it can actually take actions, not just talk
→ Docker, guardrails, and LLM-as-a-judge evals so it survives real users
→ Deployment + portfolio structure so you can actually show this work

LangChain is not the thing you need to learn.
The architecture behind it is.

I've interviewed people who could explain:
• Chains
• Agents
• Memory
• Retrievers
• Tools

But when I asked:
"How does a user query actually travel through a RAG application?"
The answers got vague.
That's the problem.

Many developers memorize LangChain components.
Few understand how they work together.

A production AI application is not:
Prompt → LLM → Done
It's usually:
Document Loader
→ Text Splitter
→ Embeddings
→ Vector Store
→ Retriever
→ Prompt
→ Model
→ Output Parser
→ Memory
→ Monitoring

Every component exists for a reason.
Bad chunking?
Retrieval quality drops.
Wrong embedding model?
Relevant documents disappear.
No memory?
Your chatbot forgets everything.
No observability?
Good luck debugging production issues.

The biggest advantage of learning LangChain isn't the framework itself.
It's learning the architecture of modern AI applications.

Because once you understand:
→ Retrieval
→ Context Management
→ Tool Calling
→ State Management
→ Agent Workflows

You can build the same systems with any framework.
LangChain.
LangGraph.
LlamaIndex.
DSPy.
Or your own code.
Frameworks change.
Architecture stays.

This carousel is one of the best visual breakdowns of how all LangChain components fit together into a real RAG application.

Save it.
You'll come back to it more than once.

GenAI Interview Question Series

Interviewer: Business stakeholders ask for measurable proof that the new assistant outperforms the old one.
Question: What offline and online evaluation metrics would you define?

Answer:
• Define offline metrics such as factual accuracy, precision, recall, F1-score, hallucination rate, and groundedness.
• Evaluate domain-specific benchmark datasets and human-annotated test cases.
• Measure latency, token consumption, and cost per request during offline testing.
• Run A/B experiments to compare both assistants in production.
• Track online KPIs including task completion rate, user satisfaction, retention, and escalation rate.
• Use dashboards combining technical and business metrics to determine whether the new model delivers measurable improvements.

𝐌𝐚𝐧𝐲 𝐩𝐞𝐨𝐩𝐥𝐞 𝐝𝐨𝐧’𝐭 𝐬𝐭𝐫𝐮𝐠𝐠𝐥𝐞 𝐰𝐢𝐭𝐡 𝐋𝐞𝐞𝐭𝐂𝐨𝐝𝐞 𝐛𝐞𝐜𝐚𝐮𝐬𝐞 𝐭𝐡𝐞𝐲 𝐥𝐚𝐜𝐤 𝐩𝐫𝐚𝐜𝐭𝐢𝐜𝐞.
They struggle because they don’t know 𝐡𝐨𝐰 𝐭𝐨 𝐜𝐡𝐨𝐨𝐬𝐞 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐫𝐢𝐠𝐡𝐭 𝐩𝐚𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐧.

That’s exactly why I created this 𝐋𝐞𝐞𝐭𝐂𝐨𝐝𝐞 𝐃𝐞𝐜𝐢𝐬𝐢𝐨𝐧 𝐓𝐫𝐞𝐞.

Instead of randomly guessing between sliding window, BFS, DFS, heaps, or dynamic programming, start with one simple question:

𝐖𝐡𝐚𝐭 𝐢𝐬 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐢𝐧𝐩𝐮𝐭?

From there, the path becomes much clearer:

• 𝐀𝐫𝐫𝐚𝐲 / 𝐒𝐭𝐫𝐢𝐧𝐠 → Two Pointers, Sliding Window, Binary Search, Heap, Intervals
• 𝐋𝐢𝐧𝐤𝐞𝐝 𝐋𝐢𝐬𝐭 → Fast & Slow Pointers, Pointer Manipulation, Two Pointers
• 𝐓𝐫𝐞𝐞 → BFS, DFS
• 𝐆𝐫𝐚𝐩𝐡 → DFS / BFS, Dijkstra, Topological Sort
• 𝐂𝐡𝐨𝐢𝐜𝐞𝐬 + 𝐑𝐞𝐩𝐞𝐚𝐭𝐞𝐝 𝐖𝐨𝐫𝐤 → Dynamic Programming

The goal is simple:
𝐒𝐭𝐨𝐩 𝐦𝐞𝐦𝐨𝐫𝐢𝐳𝐢𝐧𝐠 𝐢𝐧𝐝𝐢𝐯𝐢𝐝𝐮𝐚𝐥 𝐩𝐫𝐨𝐛𝐥𝐞𝐦𝐬.
𝐒𝐭𝐚𝐫𝐭 𝐫𝐞𝐜𝐨𝐠𝐧𝐢𝐳𝐢𝐧𝐠 𝐩𝐚𝐭𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐧𝐬.

When you can map a problem to the right pattern quickly, interviews become much less overwhelming.

My AI agent returned the right answer for two weeks. Then we looked at how it got there.

The retriever had been pulling irrelevant documents the entire time. The model just guessed well, phrased it confidently, and every check we had said "pass" because our only check was: did it return an answer?

That's the trap with LLM systems. In normal software, a bug looks like a bug: a stack trace, a red test, a null. In an agent, a bug looks like a fluent, professional, completely wrong paragraph. The failure is camouflaged inside a success.

The fix isn't more prompt-tuning. It's an evaluation layer infrastructure that scores the whole execution trace, not just the final output.

Here's the kind of thing it catches that a final-answer check never will:

➡️ Retriever pulls 3 chunks: 2 off-topic, 1 relevant
➡️ Generator writes an answer with claims that aren't in any of them
➡️ Faithfulness score: 0.42, against a 0.75 threshold
➡️ Deploy blocked, before a single user sees it

Output-only evals stamp that "pass." Trace-level evals catch it as the failure it actually is.

Most teams spend 90% of their effort building the agent and 10% checking whether it works. For anything headed to production, that ratio is backwards.

I wrote up the full setup the five ways to build an eval layer, the tooling (LangSmith, Langfuse, RAGAS, Phoenix), the metrics worth tracking, and the mistakes that make an eval layer worse than none. Link in comments.

Article: https://lnkd.in/d3Fz6RsE

Genuine question for anyone running agents in prod: what's the first thing that broke for you that a final-output check would never have flagged?



🧠 AI Made Simple: Understanding the AI Stack
Many people think LLMs, RAG, AI Agents, and MCP are the same thing—but they're not.
Here's an easy way to think about it:
🧠 LLM = Brain
Generates text and code
Understands language
Reasons based on what it has learned
📚 RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) = Brain + Books
Combines an LLM with external knowledge
Retrieves information from documents, databases, or company data
Helps reduce hallucinations and provide up-to-date answers
🤖 AI Agent = Brain + Hands
Uses an LLM to make decisions
Can use tools, APIs, memory, and workflows
Takes actions instead of only generating responses
🔗 MCP (Model Context Protocol) = Nervous System
Connects AI models with external tools and data sources
Provides a standardized way for AI applications to communicate with services
Makes AI systems more modular and scalable
💡 Together, these technologies are shaping the future of intelligent applications—from AI assistants and coding copilots to healthcare, finance, and enterprise automation.
As an AI/ML student, I'm continuously exploring these concepts and building projects that combine AI with real-world applications.
What are you currently learning or building with AI?

Few people actually understand how RAG works.

Most AI apps fail for one simple reason: they don’t know your data.

That’s where Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) changes everything.

Instead of relying only on what a model was trained on, RAG gives it access to relevant, real-time context before generating a response.

Here’s the simple flow:

✅ Indexing → Break documents into chunks and turn them into embeddings

✅ Retrieval → Find the most relevant pieces using semantic search

✅ Augmentation → Combine retrieved context with the user query

✅ Generation → Let the LLM produce a grounded, accurate answer
The result?

If you're building AI products, this isn’t optional anymore it’s foundational.

The real power of AI isn’t just generation.

It’s retrieval + generation together.

Instead of memorizing solutions, focus on recognizing patterns. That's what helps you solve unseen problems with confidence.

𝗜 𝘀𝗽𝗲𝗻𝘁 𝗵𝘂𝗻𝗱𝗿𝗲𝗱𝘀 𝗼𝗳 𝗵𝗼𝘂𝗿𝘀 𝗶𝗻 𝗴𝗲𝘁𝘁𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗴𝗼𝗼𝗱 𝗮𝘁 𝗽𝗿𝗼𝗺𝗽𝘁𝗶𝗻𝗴, 𝘁𝗵𝗲𝗻 𝗮 𝘀𝘆𝘀𝘁𝗲𝗺 𝘄𝗶𝘁𝗵 𝘄𝗼𝗿𝘀𝗲 𝗽𝗿𝗼𝗺𝗽𝘁𝘀 𝘁𝗵𝗮𝗻 𝗺𝗶𝗻𝗲.

𝗕𝗲𝗰𝗮𝘂𝘀𝗲 𝗶𝘁 𝘄𝗮𝘀𝗻'𝘁 𝗽𝗿𝗼𝗺𝗽𝘁𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗮𝘁 𝗮𝗹𝗹. 𝗜𝘁 𝘄𝗮𝘀 𝗿𝘂𝗻𝗻𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗮 𝗹𝗼𝗼𝗽. 🔁

𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝘀𝗵𝗶𝗳𝘁 𝗜 𝗸𝗲𝗲𝗽 𝗰𝗼𝗺𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗯𝗮𝗰𝗸 𝘁𝗼: the job isn't writing prompts anymore. It's designing the loop that writes them.

𝗕𝗲𝗳𝗼𝗿𝗲: you ask, read the answer, judge it, ask again. You're in control every step.
𝗔𝗳𝘁𝗲𝗿: you define the loop once — generate, score, refine, stop — and it runs that judgment without you.

𝗕𝗲𝗳𝗼𝗿𝗲: the bottleneck is you. Every iteration waits on your eyes.
𝗔𝗳𝘁𝗲𝗿: the bottleneck is the metric. The system iterates while you're doing something else.

𝗕𝗲𝗳𝗼𝗿𝗲: "good" is whatever looks good to you in the moment.
𝗔𝗳𝘁𝗲𝗿: "good" is defined up front, as a score the system can check itself against.

𝗕𝗲𝗳𝗼𝗿𝗲: you tune the wording of one request.
𝗔𝗳𝘁𝗲𝗿: you tune three things — the objective, the metric, and the stopping condition.

𝗢𝗻𝗲 𝘄𝗮𝗿𝗻𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝘁𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝘁𝗼𝗼𝗸 𝗺𝗲 𝗮 𝘄𝗵𝗶𝗹𝗲 𝘁𝗼 𝗹𝗲𝗮𝗿𝗻: If the loop grades its own work on vague criteria like taste, it just reoptimizes polish. The score climbs, the output doesn't. Wire it to a real, external signal, reply rates, conversions, something honest or it only ever repeats yesterday.

Pick one task you keep prompting by hand this week. Don't prompt it again. Instead write the loop: objective, metric, stop condition. Let it run, then tell me what broke first, that's usually where the real work is.

🚨 Open AI Most Powerful AI Model Is Here… But There's a Catch.

One of the most anticipated developments in the AI industry was the launch of OpenAI's next frontier model. That moment has now arrived—but with a significant shift in how advanced AI is being released.

Under the leadership of Sam Altman, OpenAI has introduced GPT-5.6, led by its most powerful model yet, Sol, alongside mid-tier and lower-cost versions. However, unlike previous frontier model launches, access is not broadly available. Instead, the preview has been limited to a small group of trusted partners, with reports suggesting that the U.S. government is approving access on a customer-by-customer basis, according to a reported internal memo from Sam Altman.

This follows a June 2 executive order requiring AI companies to submit advanced models for government review before public release. While OpenAI complied with the order, it also publicly objected. The launch also comes just weeks after Anthropic was required to withdraw two of its most advanced models.

For years, frontier AI models were made broadly available from day one. That trend now appears to be changing. The world's most capable AI systems are increasingly being released through controlled, gated access, with governments playing a growing role in determining when—and to whom—these models are made available.

The AI community has reacted with concern, with many questioning whether the era of open access to frontier AI is coming to an end and whether developers and businesses outside the U.S. could face increasing restrictions.

💭 What are your thoughts?

Will government oversight make frontier AI safer, or could it slow innovation and limit global access?

The difference between a 200k AI engineer and a 400k one is not how much they know about models.

It is whether they know what every call costs.

Anyone can wire up an LLM feature now. The API does the hard part. A weekend and a tutorial gets you a demo.

The expensive engineer is the one who can ship that feature to a million users without lighting your cloud bill on fire.

They think in cost per request before they think in clever. They know which calls to cache, which to batch, which to not make at all.

That skill is invisible in a demo and obvious the first month it is live.

Making it work is the junior bar now.

Making it work cheap, at scale, without a finance meeting about your inference bill, is the thing that actually pays.

𝐓𝐡𝐞 𝐭𝐞𝐫𝐦 𝐡𝐚𝐬 𝐟𝐫𝐚𝐜𝐭𝐮𝐫𝐞𝐝 𝐢𝐧𝐭𝐨 𝐭𝐡𝐫𝐞𝐞 𝐦𝐞𝐚𝐧𝐢𝐧𝐠𝐬 𝐢𝐧 𝐚𝐛𝐨𝐮𝐭 𝐚 𝐲𝐞𝐚𝐫: a formal model that infers, a SQL-native model that adopts, and a metric layer that keeps the same number consistent.
𝐓𝐡𝐞 𝐟𝐫𝐚𝐜𝐭𝐮𝐫𝐞 𝐢𝐬 𝐚 𝐦𝐚𝐩 𝐨𝐟 𝐰𝐡𝐞𝐫𝐞 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐯𝐚𝐥𝐮𝐞 𝐢𝐬 𝐦𝐨𝐯𝐢𝐧𝐠, 𝐧𝐨𝐭 𝐚 𝐯𝐨𝐜𝐚𝐛𝐮𝐥𝐚𝐫𝐲 𝐬𝐪𝐮𝐚𝐛𝐛𝐥𝐞.
